⏺ You're right, and I've corrected the research note. What I checked on GitHub
  just now:

  ┌──────┬────────────┬────────────────────────────┬─────────────┐
  │ Line │   Status   │       Latest release       │ Last commit │
  ├──────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ v2   │ stable     │ v2.18.0, 2019-12-15        │ 2021-05-13  │
  ├──────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ v3   │ alpha only │ v3.1.0-alpha.2, 2025-10-25 │ 2026-07-27  │
  └──────┴────────────┴────────────────────────────┴─────────────┘

  So the choice in Go is a stable line that has been quiet since 2021, or the
  maintained line that has not left alpha. The README, which describes v3, still
  calls the repository "beta software".

  The library status is not my main reservation about Cap'n Proto for the change
  files:

  - Its strengths are ones a log does not use. Cap'n Proto is built for reading
    a field without decoding the message, and for RPC. We read a change file
    once, front to back, and write its rows into SQLite.
  - We would have to trust a library with every stored byte. The Protocol
    Buffers byte format is small enough that we can write and fuzz the decoder
    ourselves. Cap'n Proto's is not: it has segments, pointers, far pointers,
    and defaults stored by XOR. In practice we would depend on the library and
    on the C++ schema compiler for generated code.
  - A message is built whole in memory. A commit of millions of rows needs a
    stream of many messages with our own batching.

  In its favour, the schema language and generated types are good, fields can be
  added safely, and it has a defined canonical form.

  What draws you to it? If it is the schema file and typed code, Protocol
  Buffers with generated code gives the same. If it is something else, I'd weigh
  that before we decide.

✻ Baked for 46s · done Wednesday 1:23 PM

❯ It sounds like it's Protocol Buffers but simpler, but maybe I'm mistaken.     
                                                                                
  And this paragraph on the front page:                                         
  Why do you pick on Protocol Buffers so much?                                  
                                                                                
  Because it’s easy to pick on myself. :) I, Kenton Varda, was the primary      
  author of Protocol Buffers version 2, which is the version that Google        
  released open source. Cap’n Proto is the result of years of experience        
  working on Protobufs, listening to user feedback, and thinking about how      
  things could be done better.                                                  
                                                                                
  Note that I no longer work for Google. Cap’n Proto is not, and never has      
  been, affiliated with Google.                                                 

⏺ Kenton Varda did design it as a better Protocol Buffers, but "better" meant
  faster and more capable, not a simpler byte format. The byte format got more
  complex.

  His main idea is that the bytes on the wire are the same as the layout in
  memory, so there is no encode or decode step. That makes Cap'n Proto simpler
  to use and very fast when a program reads a few fields of a large message, and
  it is the base for his RPC system. The price is that the wire format is a
  memory layout:

  ┌───────────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │               │    Protocol Buffers     │          Cap'n Proto          │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ a flat list of fields:  │ structs in 8-byte words that  │
  │ A message is  │ number, type, value     │ refer to each other by        │
  │               │                         │ pointers                      │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ A reader must │                         │ struct, list, and far         │
  │  handle       │ 4 wire types            │ pointers, segments, and       │
  │               │                         │ defaults stored by XOR        │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Skipping an   │ read its length and     │ fields past the known size    │
  │ unknown field │ skip                    │ are ignored                   │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Guarding      │                         │ check every pointer, plus     │
  │ against bad   │ check each length       │ limits on depth and total     │
  │ input         │                         │ traversal                     │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │               │ compact,                │ fixed-width, larger unless an │
  │ Size          │ variable-length         │  extra packing step is used   │
  │               │ integers                │                               │
  └───────────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  For a change file that difference decides it for me. We read every byte once,
  in order, so the missing decode step saves us nothing. And every future
  version must read these files, so I want a format whose decoder we can write,
  review, and fuzz ourselves. That is realistic for Protocol Buffers' byte
  format, and not for Cap'n Proto's, where we would rely on the Go library.

  I wrote the comparison from my knowledge of both formats, not from a fresh
  read of the Cap'n Proto encoding page. If you still lean towards it, I'd have
  that checked before we commit to anything.

  My recommendation stays the Protocol Buffers byte format, written by hand.
  Your call: that, or Cap'n Proto?

✻ Cogitated for 15s · done Wednesday 1:25 PM

❯ Okay, agreed on Protocol Buffers then. But why do we need to write it by hand 
  and not just use their tooling?                                               

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ We don't need to. Both ways write the same bytes, so this is a choice about
  our code, not about the format, and we can change it later without touching a
  stored file.

  My reasons for writing it by hand, and what each is really worth:

  - No dependency and no build tool. The tooling means Google's Go module at run
    time, and protoc when the schema changes. The generated code is committed,
    so users of s4ql need no tool. This is a real cost, but a small one: the
    module is maintained by the Go team.
  - A stricter decoder. The standard decoder accepts some odd input by design,
    such as a field that appears twice. I gave this too much weight. Every
    change file is checked against its SHA-256 name before it is decoded, so
    strictness only guards against bugs in our own writer, and we validate the
    content after decoding anyway.
  - Reading in a stream. Generated code decodes a whole message in memory. The
    tooling's answer is a file made of many small messages, which is its
    documented pattern.

  For the tooling:

  - Much less code of ours to get wrong.
  - A decoder that is fuzzed continuously and has no advisory against it.
  - The .proto file is the real schema, not documentation that can drift.
  - A tool can check in CI that a schema change does not break old files.

  Weighing it again, I'd start with the tooling. I recommended the hand-written
  route by analogy with our SQLite binding, and the analogy is weak. We wrote
  the binding to be minimal and to cross-check it against mattn's binding. Here
  the standard implementation is the trusted one, and the choice is reversible.

  Use the standard tooling?

⏺ Recorded in docs/todo.md and pushed: change files are Protocol Buffers with
  the standard tooling.

  That leaves question 2: what a change file holds, which I asked before the
  research came back. My proposal again:

  - A header: the format version, and the name of the change file before it. The
    first file has no previous name.
  - A list of changes, applied in order. Each is one of three kinds:
    - Create a table: the table's name and its columns, as data and not as SQL
      text, so that replay needs no SQL parser.
    - Put a row: the table and the full row. It replaces any row with the same
      primary key.
    - Delete a row: the table and the primary key.
  - The statements, as evidence: each statement's SQL text, its parameters, and
    its non-deterministic inputs.

  A file holds the net effect of its commit, so a primary key appears at most
  once per table.

  I left out the wall-clock time of the commit, and want you to decide that
  knowingly. Restoring a database "as it was at 14:00" would need it, and it
  cannot be added to old files later.

  Agree, and with or without the commit time?

✻ Crunched for 17s · done Wednesday 1:28 PM

❯ Okay, let's iterate on this until we both agree we're done.                   
                                                                                
  Would it make sense to make the previous name pointer required, and the first 
  just points to some magic hash number?                                        

⏺ Yes, I think it's better, for two reasons.

  - A forgotten pointer becomes an error. With an optional field, a writer bug
    that leaves previous out produces a file that looks like the first one, and
    everything before it silently disappears from the log. With a required
    field, a missing pointer is always invalid.
  - The first commit stops being a special case. If the manifest's head uses the
    same magic name for an empty log, every commit does the same thing: it sets
    previous to the head it read. Catch-up also has one rule: walk back from
    head until the cache's recorded position, which is the magic name for an
    empty cache.

  For the magic name I'd take 32 zero bytes. Git uses an all-zero name the same
  way, and it is obviously artificial. The alternative is the SHA-256 of empty
  input, which looks like a real name.

  This changes one thing we agreed an hour ago: a new database's manifest would
  have head set to 64 zeros, not left out.

  So: previous required, all zeros for the first file, and the same for head in
  the manifest?

⏺ Recorded and pushed: previous and head are required, and an empty log has the
  all-zero name.

  Next point from my side, the one I left open: does the header record the 
  wall-clock time of the commit?

  - With it, a later feature can restore or read the database "as it was at
    14:00", and a person reading the log sees when each commit happened. It is
    evidence only: nothing in replay depends on it, and it comes from the
    holder's wall clock, so it can be wrong.
  - Without it, the header holds only what replay needs. The time can be added
    as a new field later, but every file written before then stays without one.

  I'd include it. It costs eight bytes, and it is the one thing in this format
  that cannot be recovered afterwards.

  Include the commit time?

✻ Cogitated for 14s · done Wednesday 1:33 PM

❯ Yes                                                                           

⏺ Included: the header records the commit's wall-clock time, as evidence only.

  Next point: do the statements include their parameters?

  The spec says a change file stores "the SQL statements that produced them and
  their non-deterministic inputs". It does not mention parameters. For insert 
  into t1 (v1) values (?) with the value 42, the question is whether the 42 is
  stored with the statement, although the row with 42 is already in the changes.

  - With parameters, the evidence is complete. A tool can run a commit's
    statements through the engine again and compare the outcome with the
    recorded changes, which checks the engine against its own log. The cost is
    size: for inserts, the data is stored roughly twice.
  - Without parameters, files are smaller, and the statements show only the
    shape of what ran.

  I'd include them. Being able to audit the log by re-running it fits the
  project's aim of trust in correctness, and storage in S3 is cheap. If the size
  hurts later, leaving them out can become a setting.

  Include the parameters?

✻ Crunched for 20s · done Wednesday 1:34 PM

❯ Yes                                                                           

⏺ Included: each statement is stored with its parameters and its
  non-deterministic inputs.

  Next point: how do row changes say which table and columns they belong to?

  Repeating the table name in every row is wasteful, so rows are grouped by
  table. The question is what the group's heading holds.

  - A: the table name only. Rows hold their values in the table's column order.
    What that order is must be worked out from the earlier change files.
  - B: the table name and the column names. Rows hold their values in that
    order. A change file can then be read and checked without the rest of the
    log, and replay compares the heading with the table in the cache and refuses
    the file if they differ.

  I recommend B. It costs a few bytes per table per file. It makes each file
  understandable on its own, and it turns a cache that has drifted from the log
  into a loud error instead of rows written into the wrong columns. PostgreSQL's
  replication format and SQLite's changesets both put such a heading before the
  rows.

  B?
✻ Worked for 13s · done Wednesday 1:35 PM

❯ Can we step back a bit and discuss the high-level design of statements?       

⏺ Yes. Here is the design as it stands, so that we start from the same place:

  - The changes are the truth. A change file records what a commit did to the
    rows and the schema, as its net effect. Replay applies those changes and
    never runs SQL.
  - The statements are evidence. Next to the changes, the file stores the SQL
    statements that produced them, with their parameters and their
    non-deterministic inputs such as the time. Nothing depends on them. They are
    for people and tools that want to see or check what happened.
  - The two are not linked. The file does not say which statement caused which
    change. If one statement inserts a row and a later one in the same
    transaction updates it, the changes hold one row, and the statements hold
    both.

  What do you want to revisit: whether we store statements at all, how they
  relate to the changes, which statements are stored, or something else?

✻ Brewed for 9s · done Wednesday 1:36 PM

❯ Which statements. For example, you didn't mention anything about alter table  
  or drop table. Do you only want to support a subset of what's possible?       

⏺ No, not a permanent subset. I listed only what the first slice of the roadmap
  needs, which is create table and a single-row insert. The roadmap's rule is
  that nothing is supported until its slice is agreed, and I applied the same
  rule to the format: a kind of change is defined when the slice that needs it
  is built.

  So the list grows over time. drop table, alter table, and indexes each become
  a new kind of change, next to "create a table". Row changes need nothing new:
  every insert, update, and delete that we will ever support ends up as "put a
  row" and "delete a row".

  I'd wait with the others because their details depend on decisions we have not
  made. For example, when alter table adds a column with a default, the file
  can either record just the schema change, or also every existing row with its
  new value.

  Growing the list this way needs one rule that I should have stated: an 
  instance that meets a kind of change it does not know refuses the file and 
  stops replay, with an error that says a newer version of s4ql is needed. It
  must never skip it. Skipping an unknown field is safe for evidence, such as a
  new detail on a statement, but skipping a drop table would silently leave the
  cache different from the log.

  Does that answer it, and do you agree with the rule?

✻ Crunched for 36s · done Wednesday 1:46 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  I wrote the note to
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/docs/research/change-file-contents.md` and
  added a line for it to
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/docs/research/README.md`. Neither is
  committed, and nothing else was changed.

  **The open decision: the heading should hold the table name, the column names
  in order, their declared STRICT types, and the primary-key flags.** Replay
  should compare the heading with the schema it has replayed and refuse the file
  on any difference.
  - Every system that gives its reader less than column names has a documented
  way to go wrong without an error:
    - MySQL with `binlog_row_image=minimal`: the manual says source and replica
  "silently diverge".
    - SQLite's changeset (name, column count, key flags only): a table that does
  not match is "not an error", and its changes are simply not applied.
    - Turso's CDC: decoding old records "can lead to incorrect results".
  - PostgreSQL (its Relation message), Spanner (metadata in every record),
  Debezium, CockroachDB, Marmot and cr-sqlite all carry column names, and most
  carry types. MySQL made column names optional so readers can cope when the
  table differs, and they are off by default.

  **Other recommendations:**
  - Each change file stands alone. Repeat the heading after any schema change in
  the same file. PostgreSQL's schema state across messages needed special care,
  and Debezium's separate schema history causes most of its listed bugs.
  - A put always carries the full row, with no "unchanged" marker. Updating a
  primary key is a delete plus a put.
  - Schema changes are stored as data, never as DDL text to parse. Possibly also
  store the whole table definition after each change, as Debezium and Delta do.
  - `add column` with a default and `drop table` are one record each, with no
  rows repeated. SQLite allows only constant defaults in `ADD COLUMN`.
  CockroachDB re-sends every row instead, and Delta forbids the combination.
  - Replay applies results only: no triggers, cascades or row-by-row foreign-key
  checks.
  - **A real trap for "refuse unknown kinds":** with Protocol Buffers, an
  unknown kind of change looks like an empty one. The protobuf docs say there is
  "no way to tell the difference". Replay must treat a change with no known
  kind as an error.
  - Mark what a reader may skip by where it sits in the file. MySQL uses a
  single "ignorable" flag bit, and Delta separates ignorable fields from
  required features.

  **Surprising findings:**
  - Delta Lake forbids `ADD COLUMN ... DEFAULT` in one command.
  - Renaming or dropping columns broke Delta's change feed for several releases.
  - Dolt has an open issue to remove its stable numeric column ids. So I
  recommend column names plus an explicit rename record, not hidden ids.
  - rqlite still had bugs from rewriting `now` and `RANDOM()` in 2026. MySQL has
  deprecated statement-based logging.

  The note also lists seven more open decisions, among them what a schema-change
  record holds, whether to keep before-images, and how to order changes when
  one commit both alters a table and changes its rows.

  **Not verified:**
  - Whether MySQL's statement record has prepared-statement parameters filled
  in.
  - Whether a key can repeat within one transaction in Spanner, Debezium or
  CockroachDB.
  - Whether CockroachDB keeps statement text.
  - Whether SQLite's `'now'` holds one value for a whole statement.

  The helper agent read the Debezium, Spanner, CockroachDB, Dolt and
  SQLite-system sources, with citations. I re-checked only Spanner's column
  metadata and CockroachDB's re-sending of rows.

⏺ The research is in and committed as docs/research/change-file-contents.md. It
  covers PostgreSQL, MySQL, SQLite's changesets, Delta Lake, Debezium, Spanner,
  CockroachDB, Dolt, rqlite, cr-sqlite, Marmot, and Turso.

  What we agreed so far matches what the careful systems do: full rows, a
  key-only delete, schema changes as data and not as SQL text, results as the
  truth, and unknown kinds refused. The systems that replay statements instead
  have a long record of bugs. rqlite still had bugs from rewriting now and
  random() this year, and MySQL has deprecated its statement-based log.

  On the open question, the heading before a table's rows, the evidence is
  clear, and it goes one step beyond my option B:

  - Every system that gives its reader less than the column names has a 
    documented way to go wrong silently. MySQL's manual says source and replica
    "silently diverge" with minimal row images. SQLite's changesets skip a table
    that does not match and call it "not an error". Turso's documentation warns
    of "incorrect results" when decoding older records.
  - The systems built for independent readers send names and types. PostgreSQL
    sends column names, types, and key flags before the rows. Spanner and
    Debezium put them in every record.

  So the recommendation is that the heading holds the table name, the column
  names in order, each column's type, and which columns form the primary key.
  Replay compares all of it with the table in the cache and refuses the file on
  any difference. One heading covers all the rows of that table that follow it,
  so the cost is a few bytes per table per file.

  The note has more recommendations and a list of decisions that are still open.
  I'll bring those one at a time after this one.

  Heading with table name, column names, types, and primary key?

✻ Crunched for 17m 48s · done Wednesday 2:03 PM

❯ heading vs each row? Pros/cons?                                               

⏺ One heading, then many rows:

  - Smaller. The names and types are stored once per table per file. In a commit
    of a million rows, repeating them in every row would more than double the
    file.
  - Faster to replay. The instance checks the heading against the cache once,
    prepares one statement, and then only binds values row by row.
  - The cost is that a row cannot be read alone. A reader must remember the
    current heading while it reads the file. That state never crosses a file
    boundary, because every file starts fresh.

  Names and types in every row:

  - Each row is complete by itself. A reader needs no state, and rows can be
    filtered or processed separately.
  - This is why Spanner, Debezium, and CockroachDB do it: they deliver each row
    as a separate message over a stream or a queue, where a row can arrive
    alone. Our rows never travel alone. A change file is always read whole, from
    the front, after its checksum is verified.
  - The cost is size, and a check against the cache for every row.

  In both designs each value in a row already carries its own type tag: integer,
  real, text, blob, or null. So a row under a heading is not a bag of untyped
  bytes.

  I recommend the heading. PostgreSQL, MySQL, and SQLite's changesets use the
  same shape, and all three ship rows in bulk as we do.

  Heading?

⏺ Recorded and pushed. From my side the change file is now complete for the
  first slice, so here is the whole of it as a sketch. Field names and numbers
  are for the builder to finalise.

  // A change file is a sequence of Record messages, each with its length in 
  front.
  message Record {
    oneof kind {
      Header      header       = 1;  // exactly once, first
      Table       create_table = 2;  // a change
      Table       heading      = 3;  // the rows that follow belong to this 
  table
      Row         put          = 4;  // a change: all columns, in the heading's 
  order
      Row         delete       = 5;  // a change: the primary-key columns
      Statement   statement    = 6;  // evidence, may be skipped
    }
  }

  message Header {
    uint32 version  = 1;
    bytes  previous = 2;  // 32 bytes; all zero in the first file
    int64  time     = 3;  // wall-clock time of the commit; evidence
  }

  message Table {
    string name = 1;
    repeated Column columns = 2;
  }

  message Column {
    string name        = 1;
    Type   type        = 2;  // the declared type; only "int" in slice 1
    uint32 primary_key = 3;  // position in the primary key, or 0
  }

  message Row {
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  message Value {
    oneof kind {
      sint64 integer = 1;
      double real    = 2;
      bytes  text    = 3;  // bytes, because SQLite text need not be valid UTF-8
      bytes  blob    = 4;
      Null   null    = 5;
    }
  }

  message Statement {
    string sql = 1;
    repeated Value parameters = 2;
    Inputs inputs = 3;  // the time and random values the engine used
  }

  Two things to note:

  - Creating a table and the heading use the same message, because both describe
    a table: its name, columns, types, and primary key.
  - Three rules are not visible in the sketch. An unknown kind of change is
    refused. The file is checked against its SHA-256 name before it is decoded.
    Nothing carries over from one file to the next.

  Details I chose from the research are in docs/todo.md: no before-images and no
  count of changes for now, and an update of a primary key is a delete plus a
  put.

  Is anything missing or wrong for you, or are we done with the change file?
✻ Cogitated for 37s · done Wednesday 2:22 PM

❯ I don't know protobuf syntax                                                  

⏺ Then here it is without the syntax. A change file is a list of records, read
  from the front. These are the two files that the first slice's statements
  would produce.

  The commit for create table t1 (v1 int primary key) strict, without rowid:

  header        version:   1
                previous:  0000…0000        (the first file)
                time:      2026-11-11 12:00:00.123 UTC

  create table  name:      t1
                columns:   v1, type int, primary key position 1

  statement     sql:       create table t1 (v1 int primary key) strict, without 
  rowid
                parameters: none
                inputs:     none

  The commit for insert into t1 (v1) values (?) with the value 42:

  header        version:   1
                previous:  9f2c…e41a        (the name of the file above)
                time:      2026-11-11 12:00:05.456 UTC

  heading       name:      t1
                columns:   v1, type int, primary key position 1

  put           values:    integer 42

  statement     sql:       insert into t1 (v1) values (?)
                parameters: integer 42
                inputs:     none

  There are six kinds of record:

  - header: once, at the start of every file.
  - create table: a change. It holds the table's name and columns.
  - heading: says which table the following rows belong to, with the same
    content as "create table".
  - put: a change. It holds one full row, with every value tagged as integer,
    real, text, blob, or null.
  - delete: a change. It holds the primary-key values of the row to remove. No
    statement in the first slice produces one.
  - statement: evidence. It holds the SQL text, the parameters, and inputs such
    as the time the engine read.

  A commit that inserts a million rows has one heading followed by a million put
  records.

  The numbers in the earlier sketch are how Protocol Buffers identifies each
  field in the bytes. A reader skips a number it does not know, which is what
  lets the format gain fields.

  Is anything missing or wrong, or are we done with the change file?

✻ Sautéed for 10s · done Wednesday 2:23 PM

❯ Explain the protobuf instead                                                  

⏺ A .proto file describes the shape of the data. A tool generates Go types from
  it, and the library turns those into bytes and back.

  A message is a record type, like a Go struct:

  message Header {
    uint32 version  = 1;
    bytes  previous = 2;
    int64  time     = 3;
  }

  Each line inside is one field, written as type, name, number. So uint32 
  version = 1 is a field named version, of type unsigned 32-bit integer, with
  the number 1.

  The number is not a value. It is the field's identity in the bytes. The stored
  file contains "field 1 holds 1", not the name version. This is where the
  compatibility comes from:

  - A newer version adds string note = 4. An old reader sees field 4, does not
    know it, and skips it.
  - A new reader that gets an old file finds no field 4 and treats it as absent.
  - A number is never reused or changed once files exist. A name can be changed
    freely, because names are not stored.

  The types in the sketch:

  - uint32, int64: integers. sint64 is a signed integer stored so that small
    negative numbers take few bytes.
  - double: a 64-bit float.
  - string: text that must be valid UTF-8.
  - bytes: any bytes.
  - Another message's name, such as Column or Value: a nested record.

  repeated means a list:

  message Row {
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  A Row holds any number of Value records, in order.

  oneof means exactly one of several:

  message Value {
    oneof kind {
      sint64 integer = 1;
      double real    = 2;
      bytes  text    = 3;
      bytes  blob    = 4;
      Null   null    = 5;
    }
  }

  A Value is an integer or a real or a text or a blob or a null, and never two
  of them. The number that appears in the bytes tells the reader which one.
  Record uses the same construct: each record is a header or a create table or a
  heading or a put or a delete or a statement.

  Read this way, the sketch says the same as the example files: a file is a list
  of Records, and each Record is one of six kinds.

  Shall I go through the sketch message by message, or is this enough to judge
  it?

✻ Cooked for 9s · done Wednesday 2:26 PM

❯ But nothing holds the records?                                                

⏺ Right. In my sketch nothing holds them: the file itself is the container. The
  records are written one after another, each with its length in front, and the
  reader takes them off one at a time. That rule sat in a comment, outside the
  schema.

  I did it that way because of how the tooling reads. It decodes a message
  whole, into memory. If one message held all records, a commit of a million
  rows would have to fit in memory at once, and the Protocol Buffers
  documentation advises against messages larger than a few megabytes.

  There is a way to have both, and I think it is better than my sketch:

  message ChangeFile {
    repeated Record records = 1;
  }

  In the bytes, a list is stored as its items one after another, each with the
  field number and its length in front. That is almost the same as what I
  described, with one extra byte per record. So:

  - The schema describes the whole file, and no rule lives outside it.
  - A standard tool can print a whole change file, given the .proto file.
  - A small file is decoded with one call.
  - A large file is still read as a stream: the reader takes one record at a
    time and never builds the full ChangeFile in memory.

  I recommend adding the ChangeFile message.

  Agree?

⏺ Added, and recorded in docs/todo.md. The whole file:

  syntax = "proto3";

  package s4ql;

  // A change file: everything one commit did.
  // It is written and read as a stream, one record at a time.
  message ChangeFile {
    repeated Record records = 1;
  }

  // One record of a change file.
  // A reader refuses a record whose kind it does not know.
  message Record {
    oneof kind {
      Header   header       = 1; // exactly once, and first
      Table    create_table = 2; // a change: creates this table
      Table    heading      = 3; // the puts and deletes that follow belong to 
  this table
      Row      put          = 4; // a change: one full row, in the heading's 
  column order
      Row      delete       = 5; // a change: the primary-key values of the row 
  to remove
      Evidence evidence     = 6; // not needed for replay
    }
  }

  message Header {
    // The version of this format. A reader refuses a version it does not know.
    // It is raised when something is added that a reader must not ignore.
    uint32 version = 1;
    // The name of the change file before this one: 32 bytes, all zero in the 
  first file.
    bytes previous = 2;
    // The wall-clock time of the commit, in nanoseconds since 1970 UTC. 
  Evidence only.
    int64 time = 3;
  }

  // A table: what "create table" creates, and what a heading names.
  message Table {
    string name = 1;
    repeated Column columns = 2;
  }

  message Column {
    string name = 1;
    Type type = 2;
    // The column's position in the primary key, starting at 1,
    // or 0 if it is not part of the key.
    uint32 primary_key = 3;
  }

  // The declared type of a column. More are added as the roadmap grows.
  enum Type {
    TYPE_UNKNOWN = 0;
    TYPE_INT = 1;
  }

  message Row {
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  // One SQLite value.
  message Value {
    oneof kind {
      sint64 integer = 1;
      double real    = 2;
      bytes  text    = 3; // bytes, because SQLite text need not be valid UTF-8
      bytes  blob    = 4;
      Null   null    = 5;
    }
  }

  message Null {}

  // Evidence of what ran. A reader skips a kind of evidence it does not know.
  message Evidence {
    oneof kind {
      Statement statement = 1;
    }
  }

  message Statement {
    string sql = 1;
    repeated Value parameters = 2;
    // Non-deterministic inputs, such as the time, become fields here
    // when a slice of the roadmap first needs them.
  }

  Besides ChangeFile, four things differ from the first sketch:

  - Evidence wraps Statement. A reader skips an unknown kind inside Evidence and
    refuses an unknown kind inside Record. Where a kind sits then says whether
    it may be skipped.
  - The statement's inputs are gone for now. No statement in the first slice
    reads the time or a random value. They become fields when a slice needs
    them.
  - Type holds only int, following the roadmap. Value already holds all five
    kinds, because that set is SQLite's and does not change.
  - The role of version is spelled out. A new field is skipped by old readers,
    which is wrong for a field that changes replay, such as a column constraint.
    Such an addition raises version, and a reader refuses a version it does not
    know.

  Two syntax points that are new here: an enum is a fixed list of named numbers,
  and message Null {} is a record with no fields, used only as a marker.

  Anything to change, or are we done with the change file?

✻ Baked for 52s · done Wednesday 2:29 PM

❯ Do we call it Change File? Is that established terminology?                   

⏺ No. "Change file" is our own term from the spec, and I know of no system that
  uses it. The established terms for this kind of object are:

  - Commit. Git's term for an object that is named by its checksum, points to
    the one before it, and records what changed. Delta Lake also calls each file
    in its log a commit.
  - Changeset. SQLite's term for a recorded set of row changes, and also used in
    version control. For us it would be confusing: SQLite's changeset is a
    specific binary format, we use SQLite, and ours is a different format.
  - Log record, log entry, or transaction. The general database terms.
    PostgreSQL has WAL records, MySQL has binlog events, and Litestream has
    transaction files.

  I'd rename it to commit. Our design is already git's: a chain of objects named
  by checksum, and a manifest with branches and head. In git those point to
  commits, and a reader who knows git then understands our log without
  explanation. The file would be a "commit file", and the message Commit instead
  of ChangeFile.

  It has one cost: "commit" is then both the action and the stored object. Git
  has the same double meaning, and it rarely confuses anyone.

  If we rename, the spec's wording changes in about six bullets, which needs
  your agreement, and I'd also ask whether previous should become parent as in
  git.

  Rename to "commit"?

✻ Baked for 14s · done Wednesday 2:52 PM

❯ Maybe "commit object"?                                                        

⏺ Yes, that is better. "Commit object" is git's exact term for the stored thing,
  and it is also literally true for us, because it is an object in S3. It also
  removes the double meaning: "commit" is the action, and "commit object" is
  what the action stores.

  So the terms would be:

  - commit object: the immutable object in S3 that holds one commit's changes
    and evidence. It replaces "change file" everywhere.
  - commit: the action, as before.

  In the schema I'd name the top message Commit, since everything in that file
  is an object anyway.

  One follow-up before I draft the spec wording: does previous become parent, as
  in git? I'd say yes, for the same reason: the manifest already says branches
  and head, and parent completes the set. A commit object has exactly one parent
  for now, and the all-zero name for the first one.

  Rename previous to parent?

  syntax = "proto3";

  package s4ql;

  // A commit object: everything one commit did.
  // It is written and read as a stream, one record at a time.
  message Commit {
    repeated Record records = 1;
  }

  // One record of a commit object.
  // A reader refuses a record whose kind it does not know.
  message Record {
    oneof kind {
      Header   header       = 1; // exactly once, and first
      Table    create_table = 2; // a change: creates this table
      Table    heading      = 3; // the puts and deletes that follow belong to 
  this table
      Row      put          = 4; // a change: one full row, in the heading's 
  column order
      Row      delete       = 5; // a change: the primary-key values of the row 
  to remove
      Evidence evidence     = 6; // not needed for replay
    }
  }

  message Header {
    // The version of this format. A reader refuses a version it does not know.
    // It is raised when something is added that a reader must not ignore.
    uint32 version = 1;
    // The name of the commit object before this one: 32 bytes, all zero in the 
  first one.
    bytes parent = 2;
    // The wall-clock time of the commit, in nanoseconds since 1970 UTC. 
  Evidence only.
    int64 time = 3;
  }

  // A table: what "create table" creates, and what a heading names.
  message Table {
    string name = 1;
    repeated Column columns = 2;
  }

  message Column {
    string name = 1;
    Type type = 2;
    // The column's position in the primary key, starting at 1,
    // or 0 if it is not part of the key.
    uint32 primary_key = 3;
  }

  // The declared type of a column. More are added as the roadmap grows.
  enum Type {
    TYPE_UNKNOWN = 0;
    TYPE_INT = 1;
  }

  message Row {
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  // One SQLite value.
  message Value {
    oneof kind {
      sint64 integer = 1;
      double real    = 2;
      bytes  text    = 3; // bytes, because SQLite text need not be valid UTF-8
      bytes  blob    = 4;
      Null   null    = 5;
    }
  }

  message Null {}

  // Evidence of what ran. A reader skips a kind of evidence it does not know.
  message Evidence {
    oneof kind {
      Statement statement = 1;
    }
  }

  message Statement {
    string sql = 1;
    repeated Value parameters = 2;
    // Non-deterministic inputs, such as the time, become fields here
    // when a slice of the roadmap first needs them.
  }

✻ Cooked for 32s · done Wednesday 2:54 PM
❯ What does "oneof" mean in Record?                                             

⏺ oneof means that a Record holds exactly one of the six things listed, never
  two and never none.

  So each record in a commit object is a header, or a "create table", or a
  heading, or a put, or a delete, or a piece of evidence. In Go terms it is like
  an interface value that holds one of six concrete types.

  In the bytes, each record starts with the number of the field that is set. A
  reader that sees 4 knows the record is a put and that a Row follows. A reader
  that sees 1 knows it is the header.

  The word kind after oneof is only the name of the group. It is what the code
  asks: "which kind is this record?"

  The insert from the earlier example is then four records:

  Record  kind = header    →  Header    { version 1, parent 9f2c…e41a, time … }
  Record  kind = heading   →  Table     { name t1, columns: v1 int, primary key 
  1 }
  Record  kind = put       →  Row       { values: integer 42 }
  Record  kind = evidence  →  Evidence  { statement: insert into t1 (v1) values 
  (?), parameters: integer 42 }

  Value and Evidence use oneof the same way: a value is an integer or a real or
  a text or a blob or a null, and a piece of evidence is, for now, always a
  statement.

  What's next to change, or are we done?

✻ Baked for 5s · done Wednesday 2:55 PM

❯ Can we add new kinds later? Maybe subagent research protobuf best practices   

⏺ Yes. A new kind is a new line in the oneof with a new number, for example
  Table drop_table = 7. Files written before then stay valid, because they
  simply never contain a 7. A reader that is older than the new kind sees a
  record with no kind it knows, and by our rule it refuses the commit object.
  Inside Evidence the same thing is skipped instead.

  I've started a research agent on Protocol Buffers best practices.

⏺ Agent(Research protobuf best practices) Opus 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The agent checks the official guidance and reviews our draft schema against
  it. Its questions include:

  - what is safe to change once data exists, and what the official rules say
    about adding kinds to a oneof
  - how Go code tells "no kind set" from "a kind this reader does not know"
  - whether to start with proto3 or the newer "editions" syntax
  - naming, package versioning, and whether generic names such as Value and Null
    clash with Google's own types
  - whether the commit time should be Google's standard timestamp type
  - that our plan to read and write one record at a time really works, and with
    which Go functions
  - tools that check in CI that a schema change does not break stored data

  It ends with a list of changes to our draft, marked must, should, or optional,
  and a revised .proto file. I'll bring you that list when it reports, and then
  the whole file again.

✻ Sautéed for 39s · done Wednesday 2:56 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  I wrote the note at
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/docs/research/protobuf-practices.md` and
  added a line for it to
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/docs/research/README.md`. Neither is
  committed.

  **Adding `oneof` members later: yes.** Old files stay readable. A reader that
  predates a new member does not fail. It sees "no kind set" and keeps the bytes
  as an unknown field. The documentation says that in general "there is no way
  to tell the difference" between no member and an unknown member. Our case is
  different because `Record` holds nothing but its `oneof`, so any unknown field
  in it means an unknown kind. A Go test confirmed this: an unknown member
  gives `GetKind() == nil` (or `WhichKind() == Record_Kind_not_set_case`), and
  `ProtoReflect().GetUnknown()` returns the bytes with field number 7. An empty
  record has no unknown bytes. `buf breaking` passes the addition in all its
  categories. Moving an existing field into the `oneof` is unsafe.

  **Must:**
  - Add `option go_package`. The Go generator needs it.
  - Make `Statement.sql` a `bytes` field. In a test, `proto.Marshal` failed on
  SQL that was not valid UTF-8, which would fail a commit after its statements
  ran.
  - Decide what a reader does with unknown fields outside `Evidence`. Without a
  rule, an old reader replays a new column property as if it were absent. The
  choice is between refusing them (tested with a small `protoreflect` walker)
  and raising `Header.version` for every such field. This is a design question
  for Markus.
  - In the reader:
    - refuse a `Record` or `Value` with no known kind;
    - never set `DiscardUnknown`;
    - refuse unknown `Type` values, which Go keeps as plain numbers without an
  error;
    - set `protodelim` `MaxSize`, whose 4 MiB default is below SQLite's 1 GB
  value limit.

  **Should:**
  - `edition = "2024"` instead of `proto3`. It gives every field explicit
  presence and the Go API the documentation recommends, and the bytes are the
  same, so the choice can be undone. Edition 2026 is refused by `protoc-gen-go`
  v1.36.12 and only supported in v1.37.0-rc.1.
  - Package `s4ql.v1` in `s4ql/v1/`, and `TYPE_UNSPECIFIED`. These clear the
  three `buf lint` errors on the draft.
  - `google.protobuf.Timestamp` for the commit time. This is the official
  advice, and an `int64` of nanoseconds ends in 2262.
  - `buf lint` plus `buf breaking` with the `WIRE` rules in CI, a check that the
  committed generated code matches the schema, and sample commit objects from
  every format version read in tests.

  **Surprises:**
  - Names are not stored in the bytes, so they can change at any time under
  `WIRE`. Only field numbers and types are forever.
  - Two commit objects joined end to end parse as one valid `Commit`. Two
  members in one record resolve silently to the last one. So the "exactly one
  header, first" check is required.
  - The streaming plan works. Writing the byte `0x0a` and then
  `protodelim.MarshalTo` for each record gave exactly the bytes of
  `proto.Marshal(Commit)`. A file over 2 GiB can only be printed by our own
  streaming reader, not by standard tools.

  The revised `.proto` passes `buf lint` with the `STANDARD` and `COMMENTS`
  rules and generates Go code. I ran Buf v1.75.0 and `protoc-gen-go` v1.36.12
  from the scratchpad; nothing was installed system-wide.

  **Not verified:**
  - That `protoc` refuses files over 2 GiB (`protoc` was not installed).
  - Edition 2024 support in other languages' tools.
  - CockroachDB pitfalls beyond the earlier note.
  - Keyword problems with the field name `delete` outside C++ and JavaScript
  (from memory).

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The research is in and committed as docs/research/protobuf-practices.md. The
  agent ran the official lint tool on our draft and tested its claims with Go
  code.

  Adding kinds later: confirmed. A new kind is a new member of the oneof with a
  new number. Old commit objects stay valid, and the tool that checks for
  breaking changes accepts it. A Go test showed that our reader can tell a kind
  from a newer version apart from a malformed record. One thing is unsafe:
  moving an existing field into a oneof.

  The draft was sound. No message, number, or type changed. I applied these
  changes as details:

  - edition = "2024" instead of proto3. It is the current syntax. Every field
    then records whether it was set, and the bytes are the same.
  - Package s4ql.v1, and TYPE_UNSPECIFIED for the enum's zero value. Both are
    standard conventions that the lint tool requires.
  - The commit time is Google's standard timestamp type, as the official
    guidance advises.
  - The SQL text of a statement is bytes, not a string. A string field refuses
    text that is not valid UTF-8, and a commit must never fail on its evidence.
  - A comment on every element. The comments are the specification of a format
    that we keep forever.

  One useful fact: names are not stored in the bytes, so every name can still
  change later. Only numbers and types are permanent.

  // The format of an s4ql commit object.
  //
  // Commit objects are kept forever, and every version of s4ql must read
  // every one ever written. Field numbers and field types never change, and
  // a number that is no longer used is reserved. Names can change.
  edition = "2024";

  package s4ql.v1;

  import "google/protobuf/timestamp.proto";

  option go_package = "maragu.dev/s4ql/internal/s4qlpb";

  // A commit object: everything one commit did.
  //
  // It is written and read as a stream, one record at a time: each record is
  // the byte 0x0a, the length of the record as a varint, and the record.
  message Commit {
    // The records, in the order they were written.
    repeated Record records = 1;
  }

  // One record of a commit object.
  //
  // A Record holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader that finds
  // no known kind refuses the commit object: if the record holds an unknown
  // field, a newer version of s4ql wrote it; if it holds nothing, it is
  // malformed.
  message Record {
    // What the record is.
    oneof kind {
      // Exactly once, and first.
      Header header = 1;
      // A change: creates this table.
      Table create_table = 2;
      // The puts and deletes that follow belong to this table.
      Table heading = 3;
      // A change: one full row, in the heading's column order.
      Row put = 4;
      // A change: the primary-key values of the row to remove.
      Row delete = 5;
      // Not needed for replay. A reader may skip it.
      Evidence evidence = 6;
    }
  }

  // The first record of every commit object.
  message Header {
    // The version of this format. A reader refuses a version it does not know.
    // It is raised when something is added that a reader must not ignore.
    uint32 version = 1;
    // The name of the commit object before this one: 32 bytes, all zero in
    // the first one.
    bytes parent = 2;
    // The wall-clock time of the commit, from the clock of the lease holder.
    // Evidence only.
    google.protobuf.Timestamp time = 3;
  }

  // A table: what "create table" creates, and what a heading names.
  message Table {
    // The name of the table. s4ql refuses an identifier that is not valid 
  UTF-8.
    string name = 1;
    // The columns, in their declared order.
    repeated Column columns = 2;
  }

  // One column of a table.
  message Column {
    // The name of the column. s4ql refuses an identifier that is not valid 
  UTF-8.
    string name = 1;
    // The declared type of the column.
    Type type = 2;
    // The column's position in the primary key, starting at 1,
    // or 0 if it is not part of the key.
    uint32 primary_key = 3;
  }

  // The declared type of a column. More are added as the roadmap grows.
  // A reader refuses a value it does not know.
  enum Type {
    // Never written. A reader refuses it.
    TYPE_UNSPECIFIED = 0;
    // The STRICT type INT.
    TYPE_INT = 1;
  }

  // A row: its values in the heading's column order.
  message Row {
    // The values.
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  // One SQLite value.
  //
  // A Value holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader refuses a
  // value with no known kind, as it does a record.
  message Value {
    // The storage class of the value, and the value.
    oneof kind {
      // A 64-bit signed integer.
      sint64 integer = 1;
      // A 64-bit IEEE 754 floating-point number.
      double real = 2;
      // Text, as bytes, because SQLite text need not be valid UTF-8.
      bytes text = 3;
      // A blob.
      bytes blob = 4;
      // NULL.
      Null null = 5;
    }
  }

  // The SQL NULL. It has no fields.
  message Null {}

  // Evidence of what ran. A reader skips a kind of evidence it does not know.
  message Evidence {
    // What the evidence is.
    oneof kind {
      // One statement, as it ran.
      Statement statement = 1;
    }
  }

  // One statement, as it ran.
  message Statement {
    // The SQL text, as bytes, because it can hold text that is not valid UTF-8.
    bytes sql = 1;
    // The parameters, by position, starting with parameter 1.
    repeated Value parameters = 2;
    // Non-deterministic inputs, such as the time, become fields here
    // when a slice of the roadmap first needs them.
  }

  The research raised one design question for you. What does a reader do with a 
  field it does not know, outside Evidence?

  For example, a later version adds "not null" to Column. An old reader skips
  the new field by default and creates the column without the constraint, so its
  cache silently differs from the log.

  - A: the reader refuses. Any unknown field outside Evidence makes the reader
    refuse the commit object and ask for a newer s4ql. The rule is mechanical
    and needs no discipline from us. Anything that old readers may ignore goes
    into Evidence.
  - B: we raise version whenever we add a field that must not be ignored. An old
    reader then refuses by the version. This depends on us remembering it every
    time.

  I recommend A. It is the same rule we agreed for unknown kinds, applied to
  fields. version stays for a change of meaning that adds no field.

  A or B?

⏺ There are 13 inline comments, all unresolved. I'll take them in file order,
  one at a time, and change nothing until all are triaged.

  Comment 1 of 13, line 17, on the comment "each record is the byte 0x0a, the
  length of the record as a varint, and the record":

  ▎ why that byte?

  My assessment: the byte is not ours. It is how Protocol Buffers writes "here
  comes field number 1, and it has a length in front". The field number, 1, and
  the type of encoding, 2 for "length-delimited", are packed into one byte: 1 ×
  8 + 2 = 10, which is 0x0a in hexadecimal. Every item of the records list
  starts with it, because records is field 1 of the message.

  The comment is the problem. It describes the byte encoding in a place where
  the reader wants to know what the message means, and it raises exactly your
  question without answering it.

  I'd reword it to say only that the object is written and read one record at a
  time, and that each record is one item of the list. The byte-level detail then
  lives next to the code that writes it, with the explanation above.

  Reword it that way?

  Comment 3 of 13, line 33, on Header header = 1; inside Record:

  ▎ Maybe we should enforce the header in CommitObject? It is not like the 
  ▎ others

  My assessment: agree. The header is not a kind of record: it appears exactly
  once, and the others repeat. The schema should say that:

  message CommitObject {
    Header header = 1;
    repeated Record records = 2;
  }

  A commit object then has one header and a list of records, and Record loses
  its header kind. Reading and writing as a stream still works, because the
  header is simply written first.

  One limit, so that "enforce" is not overstated: Protocol Buffers can express
  "one header", but it does not reject bytes where the header is missing, or
  comes later, or appears twice. Our reader must still check that the header is
  there and comes first. The gain is that the schema now states the rule, and
  the reader checks what the schema says instead of a rule from a comment.

  Move the header out of Record into CommitObject?

✻ Cooked for 15s · done 8:58 AM

❯ yes. Can't we mark fields required or optional?                               

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Agreed and resolved: the header moves into CommitObject.

  On required and optional: not in the way you'd hope.

  - Every field is optional in the bytes. A writer can leave out any field, and
    the decoder accepts that.
  - required existed in the old syntax and was removed. The official guidance
    says "Don't add a required field", and the current syntax keeps it only for
    migrating old files. The reason, from my own knowledge of the documentation:
    a required field can never be removed or relaxed later, because old readers
    reject every message without it.
  - What we do get is presence. With the syntax we use, the generated code can
    tell for every field whether it was set, so "missing" and "zero" are
    different. Our reader then makes the check itself: no header, or a header
    without a parent, is an error.

  So the schema describes the shape, and "required" is a rule that the reader
  enforces and that the comments state.

  Comment 4 of 13, line 35, on Table create_table = 2;:

  ▎ Let's add drop table to slice 1

  My assessment: this is your call on scope, and I see no problem with it. It
  changes more than the schema:

  - The roadmap. Slice 1 gains drop table t1, with SQLite's error for a table
    that does not exist. The roadmap is a working document, so I'd update it as
    part of this.
  - The schema. A new kind of change, "drop a table", which holds only the
    table's name. Following the research, it is one record, and the rows are not
    listed as deletes.

  One thing it adds to the first slice that I think is good: a commit that
  removes data, and a table that is created again after a drop. Both exercise
  replay and the cache more than create and insert alone.

  Add drop table to slice 1, with a "drop table" kind that holds the table name?

⏺ Agreed and resolved: drop table joins slice 1.

  Comment 5 of 13, line 37, on Table heading = 3;:

  ▎ It's kinda weird that having the heading before everything else is not baked
  ▎ into the structure and enforced. Can we express that the heading must 
  ▎ always comes before the rows?

  My assessment: agree, and it can be expressed. The rows go inside one message
  together with their table:

  // Rows of one table.
  message Rows {
    Table table = 1;
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  Record then has "put rows" and "delete rows" as kinds, both holding a Rows,
  and the separate heading kind disappears. A row can no longer exist without
  its table, and the reader keeps no state from one record to the next.

  One consequence follows from how messages are written. A message has its
  length in front, so it must be complete in memory before it is written, and a
  million rows cannot go into one Rows. So a Rows is a batch with a size limit,
  of about a megabyte. A large table gets several batches, and each one repeats
  the table's description. That costs a few dozen bytes per megabyte.

  I think this is better than my heading in every respect except those few
  bytes. Each record now stands alone, which is the stronger form of the rule
  that each commit object stands alone.

  Replace the heading with a Rows batch that holds the table and its rows?

  Comment 6 of 13, line 43, on Evidence evidence = 6; inside Record:

  ▎ Also in CommitObject?

  My assessment: agree. Evidence is not like the other kinds either: the others
  are changes that replay applies and must understand, and evidence is what
  replay may skip. Putting it in its own field makes that split part of the
  structure:

  message CommitObject {
    Header header = 1;
    repeated Change changes = 2;
    repeated Evidence evidence = 3;
  }

  Two things follow:

  - Record then holds only changes, so I'd rename it to Change. Its kinds are:
    create table, drop table, put rows, delete rows.
  - The rule for readers becomes simple to state. Everything in changes must be
    understood, or the commit object is refused. Everything in evidence may be
    skipped.

  Nothing is lost. We already agreed that statements and changes are not linked,
  so their order relative to each other carries no meaning.

  Move evidence into CommitObject, and rename Record to Change?

✻ Cogitated for 16s · done 9:05 AM

❯ Why repeated Evidence? Yes to the other stuff                                 

⏺ Because one Evidence holds one piece of evidence, which today is one
  statement, and a commit can have many statements. repeated makes it a list of
  them.

  It is a list at the top level, and not one Evidence message with a list of
  statements inside, for the same reason as with the rows: a message must be
  complete in memory before it is written. A transaction with a million
  single-row inserts has a million statements. As separate items they are
  written one at a time.

  A simpler shape would be possible:

  repeated Statement statements = 3;

  That drops the Evidence wrapper. What the wrapper buys is room for other kinds
  of evidence later, which old readers know they may skip because of where they
  sit. Without it, a new kind of evidence would be a new field of CommitObject,
  and an old reader could not tell whether it may skip that.

  I'd keep the wrapper. It costs two bytes per statement, and it keeps the rule
  "everything in this field may be skipped" open for the future.

  The rest is recorded: evidence moves into CommitObject, and Record becomes
  Change.

  Keep repeated Evidence, or the simpler repeated Statement?

  Comment 7 of 13, line 51, on uint32 version = 1; in the header:

  ▎ Just a quick note: while we're developing, this is all still a draft, so

  My assessment: agree, and it matters beyond the comment. The file's opening
  lines say that numbers and types never change, which is false today. I'd
  change them to say that the rule holds from version 1 on.

  I'd also put it into the data, not only into a comment: while the format is a 
  draft, commit objects carry version 0. The first stable format is version 1. A
  draft object then says so itself, and a later release can refuse version 0
  cleanly instead of misreading an old experiment.

  The same would apply to the manifest's version, and I'd note in docs/todo.md
  that both formats are drafts until we settle on version 1, so that builders
  don't treat them as frozen.

  Comment 8 of 13, line 57, on google.protobuf.Timestamp time = 3; in the
  header, whose comment says "Evidence only":

  ▎ Then maybe it should live in Evidence?

  My assessment: agree. My own comment makes the argument: replay never uses the
  time, and it comes from a wall clock that can be wrong. Everything else like
  that sits in Evidence. Moving it there leaves the header with exactly what
  replay needs, the version and the parent.

  It becomes a second kind of evidence next to the statement:

  message Evidence {
    oneof kind {
      Statement statement = 1;
      google.protobuf.Timestamp commit_time = 2;
    }
  }

  It also shows the wrapper earning its place one comment after we kept it.

  One small cost: a tool that wants a commit's time must look through the
  evidence for it, instead of reading the first few bytes. The writer can put it
  first among the evidence, but nothing forces that. I think that is acceptable
  for something that is only informational.

✻ Brewed for 19s · done 9:12 AM

❯ Isn't time fixed per transaction in SQLite? Maybe there should be a wrapper   
  that holds "repeated Evidence", and the time alongside it?                    

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Not per transaction. SQLite fixes the time per statement. The vendored source
  says: "If the current time is requested more than once within the same run of
  a single prepared statement, the exact same time is returned for each
  invocation". Two statements in one transaction can therefore read different
  times.

  So there are two different times:

  - The time a statement read. It is an input of that statement and belongs in
    its Statement, once a slice of the roadmap supports time functions.
  - The time of the commit. It says when the commit was made. There is one per
    commit object, and this is the one we are placing now.

  On the wrapper: a message that holds all the evidence brings back the memory
  problem, because a million statements would again have to be complete before
  the wrapper is written. But CommitObject can be that wrapper. The time becomes
  its own field there, next to the evidence list:

  message CommitObject {
    Header header = 1;
    repeated Change changes = 2;
    google.protobuf.Timestamp commit_time = 3;
    repeated Evidence evidence = 4;
  }

  I think this is better than my proposal to make the time one item in the
  evidence list. The structure now says that there is one commit time, as it
  says that there is one header, and the header holds only what replay needs.
  The statements are still written one at a time.

  Put commit_time in CommitObject, next to the evidence?

✻ Cogitated for 33s · done 9:14 AM
❯ Yes                                                                           

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Agreed and resolved: commit_time is a field of CommitObject.

  Comment 9 of 13, line 76, on uint32 primary_key = 3; in Column, "the column's
  position in the primary key, starting at 1, or 0 if it is not part of the
  key":

  ▎ That's an odd way to express it. Is that an established pattern?

  My assessment: it is established, but I agree that it reads oddly, and there
  is a clearer way.

  It is how SQLite itself reports a table. pragma table_info returns a pk value
  per column that is 0 for a column outside the key and the column's position in
  the key otherwise. The vendored source computes it exactly that way. So the
  pattern has a precedent in the system we mirror.

  The clearer way is to say it as SQL does, with a list on the table:

  message Table {
    string name = 1;
    repeated Column columns = 2;
    // The names of the primary-key columns, in key order.
    repeated string primary_key = 3;
  }

  That mirrors primary key (a, b). The key and its order are stated in one
  place, there is no special meaning for 0, and it gives directly the order in
  which a "delete rows" batch lists its key values. Column then holds only a
  name and a type.

  Both forms need the same check by the reader: the key must name existing
  columns, each once.

  I'd change it to the list. Do you agree?

✻ Churned for 23s · done 9:18 AM

❯ would repeated int primary_key be better, with 0-indexed column order?        

⏺ Yes, I think it is slightly better than my list of names. A name in the key
  would be a second copy of the column's name, and a second copy can disagree
  with the first. An index cannot be misspelled, and it leads straight to the
  column.

  message Table {
    string name = 1;
    repeated Column columns = 2;
    // The primary-key columns, in key order, as positions in `columns`,
    // starting at 0.
    repeated uint32 primary_key = 3;
  }

  The type is uint32, because Protocol Buffers has no plain int, and a position
  is never negative.

  The cost is that a printed commit object shows primary_key: [0] and not
  primary_key: ["v1"], so a person must count columns. That is minor.

  The reader checks that every position exists in columns and appears once.

  Go with the list of positions?

❯ Yes. Remember, we don't optimize this for printing, but for robustness,       
  consistency, speed, and other things. But printing to screen is not one of    
  them.

  Comment 10 of 13, line 102, on sint64 integer = 1; in Value:

  ▎ Do you know why strict tables support both int and integer? What's the 
  ▎ difference? https://sqlite.org/stricttables.html

  My assessment: I read that page and the create table page just now. There is
  exactly one difference, and it does not apply to us.

  - The strict tables page says: "An INTEGER PRIMARY KEY column is an alias for
    the rowid, but an INT PRIMARY KEY column is not."
  - The create table page says a primary-key column becomes that alias only "if
    the declared type name is exactly "INTEGER"". Any other name, such as INT,
    gives "an ordinary table column with integer affinity and a unique index".

  So the two names store the same values, and differ only in whether a
  single-column primary key becomes the table's internal row number. The
  documentation does not say why strict tables kept both. My reading is that it
  preserves this old quirk, so that a user can choose either behaviour.

  All our tables are WITHOUT ROWID, and such tables have no row number at all.
  For s4ql, int and integer therefore behave the same.

  For the schema this means no change now. Value.integer is the stored value and
  is the same for both. The Type enum has only TYPE_INT, because slice 1
  accepts only int. Whether integer later becomes a second value in the enum or
  is treated as the same type is a question for the slice that adds it.

  No change for this comment?

✻ Crunched for 42s · done 9:21 AM

❯ Maybe call the field "int"?                                                   

⏺ I'd keep integer, for two reasons.

  - It is SQLite's name for the stored value. The five kinds in Value are
    SQLite's storage classes, and SQLite calls them NULL, INTEGER, REAL, TEXT,
    and BLOB. typeof(42) returns integer. Our five field names match those
    exactly.
  - It keeps two different things apart. int is a declared column type, which is
    what TYPE_INT in the Type enum records. integer is what a value is. A
    column declared int holds values of the storage class integer, and so would
    a column declared integer. If the value field were also called int, the
    schema would suggest that the two are the same thing.

  A smaller point: int is a reserved word in several languages that code can be
  generated for. The practices research flagged delete for the same reason.

  Names are not stored in the bytes, so this can be changed at any time without
  touching a commit object. It is your call.

  Keep integer, or rename to int?

✻ Brewed for 7s · done 9:24 AM

❯ I'd just like to be consistent and aligned with SQLite if possible. (Why is   
  null a type?)                                                                 

⏺ Fetch(https://sqlite.org/datatype3.html)
  ⎿  Received 37.8KB (200 OK)

⏺ Then integer is the aligned choice, and so are the other four. SQLite's
  documentation on datatypes says: "Each value stored in an SQLite database (or
  manipulated by the database engine) has one of the following storage classes",
  and lists NULL, INTEGER, REAL, TEXT, and BLOB. Our Value has exactly those
  five, under the same names.

  That list also answers your second question. In SQLite, NULL is not the
  absence of a value. It is one of the five storage classes: "NULL. The value is
  a NULL value." typeof(null) returns 'null', as typeof(42) returns 'integer'.

  The schema has a second reason to store it explicitly. If NULL were written as
  "no kind set", it would look the same as a kind from a newer version that
  this reader does not know, and that case must be refused. With an explicit
  null, the three cases stay apart:

  - null is set: the value is NULL.
  - An unknown field is there: a newer version wrote a kind this reader does not
    know, so it refuses.
  - Nothing is there: the value is malformed, so it refuses.

  The message Null exists only so that the oneof has something to hold for that
  case. It has no fields.

  So for comment 10: no change, and integer stays because it is SQLite's name.
  Agreed?

⏺ Agreed and resolved: no change, integer stays.

  Comment 11 of 13, line 105, on "Text, as bytes, because SQLite text need not
  be valid UTF-8":

  ▎ Should we enforce that it should be in our subset?

  My assessment: yes. I think this is a strong improvement, and it reaches
  beyond the schema.

  - It fits the roadmap's rule, "reject, never reinterpret". SQLite accepts text
    that is not valid UTF-8. We would reject it with an error, and everything
    we accept still behaves exactly as in SQLite.
  - It removes a whole class of oracle work. Our engine must give SQLite's
    results. For broken text, the results of functions such as length and substr
    depend on quirks of SQLite's decoder, and we would have to copy every one
    of them. With valid text only, those cases cannot occur.
  - The schema gets stricter for free. Value.text becomes a string. Protocol
    Buffers refuses a string that is not valid UTF-8 both when writing and when
    reading. If a bug of ours let broken text through, the commit would fail
    before the manifest swap, so nothing bad is committed.

  What it needs: a new line under "Restrictions" in the roadmap, saying that
  text is always valid UTF-8, and that a parameter or an expression that would
  produce other text is rejected. The engine must check wherever bytes become
  text, for example a cast from a blob.

  Enforce valid UTF-8 for text, make Value.text a string, and add the
  restriction to the roadmap?

✻ Cogitated for 26s · done 9:28 AM

❯ yes                                                                           

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Agreed and resolved: text is always valid UTF-8, and Value.text becomes a
  string.

  Comment 12 of 13, line 118, on message Evidence {:

  ▎ I don't like the name. Can we call it something else?

  My assessment: fine with me. The name comes from the spec's sentence that
  statements "are evidence, not the truth", and it does sound like a courtroom.
  What the name has to carry is: these items say something about the commit, and
  replay may skip them. Three candidates:

  - Annotation. An annotation is attached to something without changing what it
    means, which is exactly the rule for readers. It also works as a countable
    item: repeated Annotation annotations, where each one is a statement today.
    Kubernetes uses the word for metadata that tools may ignore.
  - Metadata. The plain word, and the spec's bullet is already titled
    "Statements are kept as metadata". It is vague, though: the header's version
    and parent are metadata too, and those must not be skipped.
  - Provenance. The precise term for a record of how data came to be. It fits
    the statements well, but it is not countable, and it is not a plain word.

  I'd take Annotation. Its everyday meaning matches the rule that matters, which
  is that a reader loses nothing essential by ignoring it.

  Whichever you choose, I'd use the same word in the spec when we reword it, so
  that one thing has one name.

  Annotation, one of the others, or a word of your own?

  Comment 13 of 13, line 128, on "The SQL text, as bytes, because it can hold

  ▎ Let's restrict ourselves to valid UTF-8? I have a hunch that's going to make

  My assessment: agree. It follows from comment 11, and I think your hunch is
  right.

  - s4ql rejects a statement whose SQL text is not valid UTF-8, before it parses
    or runs anything.
  - Statement.sql becomes a string. I made it bytes so that a commit could never
    fail on its annotation after its statements had run. With the rejection at
    the door, that case cannot arise.
  - Every text in a commit object is then a string: table names, column names,
    text values, and SQL text. There is one rule and no exceptions, and the
    parser, the engine, and every tool can rely on it.

  The roadmap already says "UTF-8 only" under "Restrictions". I'd make that line
  precise: SQL text, identifiers, and text values are valid UTF-8, and anything
  else is rejected.

  Restrict SQL text to valid UTF-8 and make Statement.sql a string?
