⏺ All 13 comments are applied and pushed to the pull request:
  https://github.com/maragudk/s4ql/pull/4. The file is now
  proto/s4ql/v1/commit_object.proto, and it passes Buf's lint. On main I
  recorded the decisions in docs/todo.md, and updated docs/roadmap.md with drop 
  table in slice 1 and the precise UTF-8 restriction.

  The whole file:

  // The format of an s4ql commit object.
  //
  // The format is a draft while s4ql is in development. Anything in it can
  // change, including field numbers and types, and a commit object written
  // in it carries version 0.
  //
  // From version 1 on, commit objects are kept forever, and every version of
  // s4ql must read every one ever written. Field numbers and field types then
  // never change, and a number that is no longer used is reserved. Names can
  // change.
  edition = "2024";

  package s4ql.v1;

  import "google/protobuf/timestamp.proto";

  option go_package = "maragu.dev/s4ql/internal/s4qlpb";

  // A commit object: everything one commit did.
  //
  // It is written and read as a stream: the header first, then the changes
  // and the annotations, one at a time.
  message CommitObject {
    // Exactly once, and first. A reader refuses a commit object without it.
    Header header = 1;
    // What the commit changed, in the order in which replay applies it.
    // A reader refuses the commit object if it does not know a change.
    repeated Change changes = 2;
    // The wall-clock time of the commit, from the clock of the lease holder.
    // Replay does not use it.
    google.protobuf.Timestamp commit_time = 3;
    // What else is known about the commit. Replay does not use them, and a
    // reader may skip them.
    repeated Annotation annotations = 4;
  }

  // What a reader needs before it reads anything else.
  message Header {
    // The version of this format: 0 while the format is a draft. A reader
    // refuses a version it does not know. From version 1 on, the version is
    // raised when something is added that a reader must not ignore.
    uint32 version = 1;
    // The name of the commit object before this one: 32 bytes, all zero in
    // the first one.
    bytes parent = 2;
  }

  // One change to the database.
  //
  // A Change holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader that finds
  // no known kind refuses the commit object: if the change holds an unknown
  // field, a newer version of s4ql wrote it; if it holds nothing, it is
  // malformed.
  message Change {
    // What the change is.
    oneof kind {
      // Creates this table.
      Table create_table = 1;
      // Drops this table and all its rows.
      DropTable drop_table = 2;
      // Puts rows. Each row holds a value for every column, in the table's
      // column order, and replaces any row with the same primary key.
      Rows put_rows = 3;
      // Deletes rows. Each row holds only its primary-key values, in key
      // order.
      Rows delete_rows = 4;
    }
  }

  // A table.
  message Table {
    // The name of the table.
    string name = 1;
    // The columns, in their declared order.
    repeated Column columns = 2;
    // The primary-key columns, in key order, as positions in `columns`,
    // starting at 0.
    repeated uint32 primary_key = 3;
  }

  // One column of a table.
  message Column {
    // The name of the column.
    string name = 1;
    // The declared type of the column.
    Type type = 2;
  }

  // The declared type of a column. More are added as the roadmap grows.
  // A reader refuses a value it does not know.
  enum Type {
    // Never written. A reader refuses it.
    TYPE_UNSPECIFIED = 0;
    // The STRICT type INT.
    TYPE_INT = 1;
  }

  // The table to drop.
  message DropTable {
    // The name of the table.
    string name = 1;
  }

  // Rows of one table, together with the table they belong to.
  //
  // A Rows is a batch that is small enough to hold in memory. A commit that
  // changes many rows of one table writes several batches, and each one holds
  // the table again, so a batch is read without the ones before it.
  message Rows {
    // The table. A reader refuses the commit object if this differs from the
    // table it has.
    Table table = 1;
    // The rows.
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  // One row: its values, in the order that the change gives.
  message Row {
    // The values.
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  // One SQLite value, in one of SQLite's five storage classes.
  //
  // A Value holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader refuses a
  // value with no known kind, as it does a change.
  message Value {
    // The storage class of the value, and the value.
    oneof kind {
      // A 64-bit signed integer.
      sint64 integer = 1;
      // A 64-bit IEEE 754 floating-point number.
      double real = 2;
      // Text. It is always valid UTF-8.
      string text = 3;
      // A blob.
      bytes blob = 4;
      // NULL.
      Null null = 5;
    }
  }

  // The SQL NULL. It has no fields.
  message Null {}

  // One thing that is known about a commit beyond its changes.
  //
  // A reader skips a kind of annotation it does not know.
  message Annotation {
    // What the annotation is.
    oneof kind {
      // One statement, as it ran.
      Statement statement = 1;
    }
  }

  // One statement, as it ran.
  message Statement {
    // The SQL text. It is always valid UTF-8.
    string sql = 1;
    // The parameters, by position, starting with parameter 1.
    repeated Value parameters = 2;
    // Non-deterministic inputs, such as the time the statement read, become
    // fields here when a slice of the roadmap first needs them.
  }

  Two things I chose while applying the comments:

  - drop_table holds a small DropTable message with the name, not a bare string,
    so that it can gain fields like every other kind.
  - The package is still named s4ql.v1, which the lint tool requires, although
    the format version in the data is 0 while it is a draft. The package name is
    not stored in the bytes.

  One question from before the review is still open: what a reader does with a
  field it does not know outside the annotations, such as a later "not null" on
  a column. I recommend that it refuses the commit object.

  You can comment on the new version in the pull request, or tell me here.
  What's next, or are we done with the schema?

✻ Cogitated for 2m 28s · done 10:01 AM

⏺ Two new threads, both unresolved. Same procedure: one at a time, and no

  Comment 1 of 2, line 31, on google.protobuf.Timestamp commit_time = 3; in
  CommitObject:

  ▎ Should we put these before changes, maybe even in a metadata wrapper, so we 
  ▎ can add more later?
  ▎
  ▎ or maybe the commit timestamp is just a part of the header

  My assessment: I'd take your second thought and put it in the header.

  message Header {
    uint32 version = 1;
    bytes parent = 2;
    google.protobuf.Timestamp commit_time = 3;
  }

  - The header describes the commit object: which format it is in, what came
    before it, and when it was made. Git's commit header holds the parent and
    the time in the same way. That replay ignores the time does not make it a
    stranger there.
  - It is the simplest shape. The time comes before the changes, the structure
    says there is one, and no new message is needed.
  - I argued against this in the last round, and I think I was wrong. I wanted
    the header to hold only what replay needs. That is a tidy rule, but it
    bought us nothing and cost a field in an odd place.

  On the metadata wrapper: I'd wait. Today it would hold one field. When a
  second informational fact about a commit turns up, such as the s4ql version
  that wrote it, we can add it to the header or make it a kind of annotation,
  and the format is a draft until then.

  I'd leave the annotations after the changes. They are the statements, which
  can be the largest part, and replay does not need them.

  Move commit_time into the header?

✻ Worked for 34s · done 10:07 AM

❯ Yes                                                                           

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Agreed and resolved: commit_time moves into the header.

  Comment 2 of 2, line 58, on Table create_table = 1;:

  ▎ Should be called CreateTable type
  ▎
  ▎ Or at least be consistent. drop_table could also use Table message?

  My assessment: you're right that it is inconsistent, and I'd take both of your
  suggestions together. Every kind of change gets a message named after it, and
  every one of them holds the table in full:

  message CreateTable {
    Table table = 1;
  }

  message DropTable {
    Table table = 1;
  }

  message PutRows {
    Table table = 1;
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  message DeleteRows {
    Table table = 1;
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  - One naming rule: the kind create_table holds a CreateTable, drop_table a
    DropTable, and so on. The shared Rows message goes away.
  - Each kind can gain fields without touching the others. For example, "delete
    rows" could later carry the old rows, which "put rows" never needs.
  - Every change starts with its table, and replay always checks it. For a
    create, the table must not exist. For a drop and for rows, the table in the
    cache must match the one in the change. A drop that names only the table
    would be the one change that skips this check. With the full table, a cache
    that has drifted from the log is caught there too.

  The cost is that a drop carries the column list, which is a few bytes.

  Give every kind its own message, each holding the full Table?

✻ Brewed for 22s · done 10:13 AM

❯ Do we even need the Table message then? Why not just inline into each the     
  fields we need?                                                               

⏺ We could, but I'd keep Table, because all four need the same three fields: the
  name, the columns, and the primary key.

  Inlined, each of the four messages would repeat them:

  message DropTable {
    string name = 1;
    repeated Column columns = 2;
    repeated uint32 primary_key = 3;
  }
  // ...and the same three lines in CreateTable, PutRows, and DeleteRows

  That has two costs:

  - A later addition must be made in four places. If a table gains a property,
    such as a table-level constraint, four messages change together, and nothing
    stops one of them from being forgotten.
  - The code needs one check, not four. Replay compares "the table in the
    change" with "the table in the cache". With one Table type that is one
    function. With inlined fields the generated code has four unrelated types
    that happen to have the same fields.

  Table also says something true: these three things together are what a table
  is, in every place where one appears.

  Inlining would be right if the kinds needed different fields, for example if a
  drop held only the name. But then the drop would lose the check against the
  cache, which is why I proposed the full table for it.

  The nesting costs two bytes per change.

  Keep Table, with the four messages as proposed?

✻ Cooked for 9s · done 10:15 AM

❯ But e.g. DropTable doesn't need columns nor primary key. That's my point.     

⏺ You're right, and following your point through gives a better design than
  mine.

  I wanted the full table in a drop so that replay could check it against the
  cache. That check protects nothing. A drop removes the table whatever its
  columns are, so it cannot write wrong data. The check matters for rows, where
  values go into columns.

  If each message holds only what it needs, the row messages get simpler too,
  and Table disappears:

  message CreateTable {
    string table_name = 1;
    repeated Column columns = 2;
    // The primary-key columns, in key order, as positions in `columns`.
    repeated uint32 primary_key = 3;
  }

  message DropTable {
    string table_name = 1;
  }

  message PutRows {
    string table_name = 1;
    // The columns of the values in each row: all columns of the table.
    repeated Column columns = 2;
    repeated Row rows = 3;
  }

  message DeleteRows {
    string table_name = 1;
    // The columns of the values in each row: the primary-key columns.
    repeated Column columns = 2;
    repeated Row rows = 3;
  }

  - In the row messages, columns describes exactly the values in each row, one
    column per value. For a put that is every column of the table. For a delete
    it is only the key columns, in key order.
  - The primary key is stated once, where the table is created. A put does not
    need it, and a delete shows it through its columns.
  - Replay still checks before it writes. For a put, the columns must equal the
    table's columns. For a delete, they must equal its key columns.

  I used table_name in all four so that the field has one name everywhere.

  Go with these four messages, without Table?

✻ Baked for 20s · done 10:16 AM

❯ Why does DeleteRows need columns? Why not just primary keys?                  

⏺ It needs them for the same reason that a put does: so that replay can check
  before it acts.

  A delete row holds only values, for example (7, 3). Which columns those belong
  to is decided by position. Without columns, replay matches them against the
  primary key of the table in the cache and trusts that the writer meant the
  same key in the same order.

  If that trust is ever wrong, the result is silent. Say the writer's key was
  (a, b) and the cache's table has the key (b, a). The delete of (7, 3) then
  removes the row where b = 7 and a = 3, which is a different row, or none. No
  error occurs.

  With columns, the delete says "these values are a and b, in that order".
  Replay compares that with the table's key and refuses the commit object if
  they differ. That is the same check as for a put, where a mismatch would write
  values into the wrong columns.

  So strictly, a delete can be applied without them. What they buy is that a
  cache which has drifted from the log is caught, and not acted on. The research
  found that every system that left this out has a documented silent failure of
  this kind. The cost is one small list per batch, not per row.

  I'd keep them, for that check and so that puts and deletes work the same way.

  Keep columns in DeleteRows?

✻ Cooked for 12s · done 10:18 AM

❯ I don't understand why we need the check. The precondition is that the cache  
  is correct up to the parent, and with a deterministic change, the commit is   
  safe and the postcondition is the same. No?                                   

⏺ Yes, that reasoning holds, and it shows the check is redundant.

  The cache records the name of the last commit object it applied, and replay
  only applies a commit object whose parent is that name. That is the
  precondition, and it is already checked. Given it, the table in the cache is
  the table the writer saw, so the column check can never fail. It could only
  fire when the precondition is already broken, which means a bug of ours or
  someone changing the cache file from outside.

  I leaned on the research too hard. Its silent failures come from MySQL
  replicas and SQLite changesets, where the reader's tables are managed
  separately from the writer's and really can differ. Our cache is private and
  is written only by replay, so that evidence does not carry over.

  So the columns would be a second assertion of something we already guarantee,
  at the cost of more format to keep compatible. By your own rule, the
  essentials, they go. And if a delete doesn't need them, a put doesn't either:

  message PutRows {
    string table_name = 1;
    // Each row holds a value for every column, in the table's column order.
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  message DeleteRows {
    string table_name = 1;
    // Each row holds the primary-key values, in key order.
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  Two checks remain without any extra data: a row with the wrong number of
  values is refused, and the cache's strict tables reject a value of the wrong
  type.

  This reverses what we agreed yesterday about the heading holding column names
  and types. The table's definition then appears once in the log, in
  CreateTable.

  Drop columns from both PutRows and DeleteRows?
✻ Cooked for 25s · done 10:20 AM

❯ Yes. And table_name -> name?                                                  

⏺ Recorded: no columns in PutRows and DeleteRows.

  On the field name: name reads well in CreateTable and DropTable, where the
  message is about a table. In PutRows and DeleteRows it reads less well,
  because a batch of rows has no name of its own, so name makes the reader ask
  "name of what?".

  A word that works in all four is table:

  message CreateTable { string table = 1; ... }
  message DropTable   { string table = 1; }
  message PutRows     { string table = 1; ... }
  message DeleteRows  { string table = 1; ... }

  Each then answers "which table?" the same way. I'd take table, but name
  everywhere is also consistent, and names can be changed at any time because
  they are not stored in the bytes.

  table or name?

⏺ Both comments and the table_name rename are applied and pushed to the pull
  request, and recorded in docs/todo.md. The file passes Buf's lint.

  The whole file:

  // The format of an s4ql commit object.
  //
  // The format is a draft while s4ql is in development. Anything in it can
  // change, including field numbers and types, and a commit object written
  // in it carries version 0.
  //
  // From version 1 on, commit objects are kept forever, and every version of
  // s4ql must read every one ever written. Field numbers and field types then
  // never change, and a number that is no longer used is reserved. Names can
  // change.
  edition = "2024";

  package s4ql.v1;

  import "google/protobuf/timestamp.proto";

  option go_package = "maragu.dev/s4ql/internal/s4qlpb";

  // A commit object: everything one commit did.
  //
  // It is written and read as a stream: the header first, then the changes
  // and the annotations, one at a time.
  message CommitObject {
    // Exactly once, and first. A reader refuses a commit object without it.
    Header header = 1;
    // What the commit changed, in the order in which replay applies it.
    // A reader refuses the commit object if it does not know a change.
    repeated Change changes = 2;
    // What else is known about the commit. Replay does not use them, and a
    // reader may skip them.
    repeated Annotation annotations = 3;
  }

  // What the commit object is: its format, what came before it, and when it
  // was made.
  message Header {
    // The version of this format: 0 while the format is a draft. A reader
    // refuses a version it does not know. From version 1 on, the version is
    // raised when something is added that a reader must not ignore.
    uint32 version = 1;
    // The name of the commit object before this one: 32 bytes, all zero in
    // the first one.
    bytes parent = 2;
    // The wall-clock time of the commit, from the clock of the lease holder.
    // Replay does not use it.
    google.protobuf.Timestamp commit_time = 3;
  }

  // One change to the database.
  //
  // A Change holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader that finds
  // no known kind refuses the commit object: if the change holds an unknown
  // field, a newer version of s4ql wrote it; if it holds nothing, it is
  // malformed.
  message Change {
    // What the change is.
    oneof kind {
      // Creates a table.
      CreateTable create_table = 1;
      // Drops a table and all its rows.
      DropTable drop_table = 2;
      // Puts rows into a table.
      PutRows put_rows = 3;
      // Deletes rows from a table.
      DeleteRows delete_rows = 4;
    }
  }

  // Creates a table.
  message CreateTable {
    // The name of the table.
    string table_name = 1;
    // The columns, in their declared order.
    repeated Column columns = 2;
    // The primary-key columns, in key order, as positions in `columns`,
    // starting at 0.
    repeated uint32 primary_key = 3;
  }

  // One column of a table.
  message Column {
    // The name of the column.
    string name = 1;
    // The declared type of the column.
    Type type = 2;
  }

  // The declared type of a column. More are added as the roadmap grows.
  // A reader refuses a value it does not know.
  enum Type {
    // Never written. A reader refuses it.
    TYPE_UNSPECIFIED = 0;
    // The STRICT type INT.
    TYPE_INT = 1;
  }

  // Drops a table and all its rows.
  message DropTable {
    // The name of the table.
    string table_name = 1;
  }

  // Puts rows into a table. A row replaces any row with the same primary key.
  //
  // A PutRows is a batch that is small enough to hold in memory. A commit that
  // puts many rows into one table writes several batches.
  message PutRows {
    // The name of the table.
    string table_name = 1;
    // The rows. Each holds a value for every column, in the table's column
    // order.
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  // Deletes rows from a table.
  //
  // A DeleteRows is a batch, like a PutRows.
  message DeleteRows {
    // The name of the table.
    string table_name = 1;
    // The rows. Each holds only its primary-key values, in key order.
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  // One row: its values, in the order that the change gives.
  message Row {
    // The values.
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  // One SQLite value, in one of SQLite's five storage classes.
  //
  // A Value holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader refuses a
  // value with no known kind, as it does a change.
  message Value {
    // The storage class of the value, and the value.
    oneof kind {
      // A 64-bit signed integer.
      sint64 integer = 1;
      // A 64-bit IEEE 754 floating-point number.
      double real = 2;
      // Text. It is always valid UTF-8.
      string text = 3;
      // A blob.
      bytes blob = 4;
      // NULL.
      Null null = 5;
    }
  }

  // The SQL NULL. It has no fields.
  message Null {}

  // One thing that is known about a commit beyond its changes.
  //
  // A reader skips a kind of annotation it does not know.
  message Annotation {
    // What the annotation is.
    oneof kind {
      // One statement, as it ran.
      Statement statement = 1;
    }
  }

  // One statement, as it ran.
  message Statement {
    // The SQL text. It is always valid UTF-8.
    string sql = 1;
    // The parameters, by position, starting with parameter 1.
    repeated Value parameters = 2;
    // Non-deterministic inputs, such as the time the statement read, become
    // fields here when a slice of the roadmap first needs them.
  }

  What changed since the last version:

  - commit_time is in the header, and CommitObject has three fields.
  - Each kind of change has its own message, CreateTable, DropTable, PutRows,
    and DeleteRows, and each holds only what it needs. Table and Rows are gone.
  - Row changes carry no column names or types.
  - The table field is table_name in all four.

  A follow-up to your point about SQLite's names: its grammar also says
  column-name and type-name, and our Column has name and type. For the same
  alignment these would become column_name and type_name. I have not changed
  them.

  Rename Column's fields to column_name and type_name as well?

  The whole file:

  // The format of an s4ql commit object.
  //
  // The format is a draft while s4ql is in development. Anything in it can
  // change, including field numbers and types, and a commit object written
  // in it carries version 0.

  // A commit object: everything one commit did.
  //

  // and the annotations, one at a time.

    Header header = 1;

    // A reader refuses the commit object if it does not know a change.
    repeated Change changes = 2;
    // What else is known about the commit. Replay does not use them, and a
    // reader may skip them.
    repeated Annotation annotations = 3;
  }

  // What the commit object is: its format, what came before it, and when it
  // was made.
  message Header {
    // The version of this format: 0 while the format is a draft. A reader
    // refuses a version it does not know. From version 1 on, the version is
    // raised when something is added that a reader must not ignore.
    uint32 version = 1;

    // the first one.
    bytes parent = 2;
    // The wall-clock time of the commit, from the clock of the lease holder.
    // Replay does not use it.
    google.protobuf.Timestamp commit_time = 3;
  }

  // One change to the database.
  //
  // A Change holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader that finds
  // no known kind refuses the commit object: if the change holds an unknown
  // field, a newer version of s4ql wrote it; if it holds nothing, it is
  // malformed.
  message Change {
    // What the change is.
    oneof kind {
      // Creates a table.
      CreateTable create_table = 1;

      DropTable drop_table = 2;
      // Puts rows into a table.
      PutRows put_rows = 3;
      // Deletes rows from a table.
      DeleteRows delete_rows = 4;
    }
  }

  // Creates a table.
  message CreateTable {
    // The name of the table.
    string table_name = 1;
    // The columns, in their declared order.
    repeated Column columns = 2;
    // The primary-key columns, in key order, as positions in `columns`,
    // starting at 0.
    repeated uint32 primary_key = 3;
  }

  // One column of a table.
  message Column {
    // The name of the column.
    string column_name = 1;
    // The declared type of the column.
    TypeName type_name = 2;
  }

  // The declared type of a column. More are added as the roadmap grows.
  // A reader refuses a value it does not know.
  enum TypeName {
    // Never written. A reader refuses it.
    TYPE_NAME_UNSPECIFIED = 0;
    // The STRICT type INT.
    TYPE_NAME_INT = 1;
  }

  // Drops a table and all its rows.
  message DropTable {
    // The name of the table.
    string table_name = 1;
  }

  // Puts rows into a table. A row replaces any row with the same primary key.
  //
  // A PutRows is a batch that is small enough to hold in memory. A commit that
  // puts many rows into one table writes several batches.
  message PutRows {
    // The name of the table.
    string table_name = 1;
    // The rows. Each holds a value for every column, in the table's column
    // order.
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  // Deletes rows from a table.
  //
  // A DeleteRows is a batch, like a PutRows.

    // The name of the table.
    string table_name = 1;
    // The rows. Each holds only its primary-key values, in key order.
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  // One row: its values, in the order that the change gives.
  message Row {
    // The values.
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  // One SQLite value, in one of SQLite's five storage classes.
  //
  // A Value holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader refuses a
  // value with no known kind, as it does a change.
  message Value {
    // The storage class of the value, and the value.
    oneof kind {
      // A 64-bit signed integer.
      sint64 integer = 1;
      // A 64-bit IEEE 754 floating-point number.
      double real = 2;
      // Text. It is always valid UTF-8.
      string text = 3;
      // A blob.
      bytes blob = 4;
      // NULL.
      Null null = 5;
    }
  }

  // The SQL NULL. It has no fields.
  message Null {}

  // One thing that is known about a commit beyond its changes.
  //
  // A reader skips a kind of annotation it does not know.
  message Annotation {
    // What the annotation is.
    oneof kind {
      // One statement, as it ran.
      Statement statement = 1;
    }
  }

  // One statement, as it ran.
  message Statement {
    // The SQL text. It is always valid UTF-8.
    string sql = 1;
    // The parameters, by position, starting with parameter 1.
    repeated Value parameters = 2;
    // Non-deterministic inputs, such as the time the statement read, become
    // fields here when a slice of the roadmap first needs them.
  }

  The question from before the review is still open: what a reader does with a
  field it does not know outside the annotations, such as a later "not null" on
  a column. I recommend that it refuses the commit object.

  Anything more on the schema, or shall we settle that question next?

  passes Buf's lint.

  I read "table, column, type" as the names from before: table in the four
  change messages, and name and type inside Column, with the enum back to Type.
  A field named column inside the message Column would read as Column.column, so
  I kept name there. Tell me if you meant something else.

  The whole file:

  // The format of an s4ql commit object.
  //
  // The format is a draft while s4ql is in development. Anything in it can
  // change, including field numbers and types, and a commit object written
  // in it carries version 0.
  //
  // From version 1 on, commit objects are kept forever, and every version of
  // s4ql must read every one ever written. Field numbers and field types then
  // never change, and a number that is no longer used is reserved. Names can
  // change.
  edition = "2024";

  package s4ql.v1;

  import "google/protobuf/timestamp.proto";

  option go_package = "maragu.dev/s4ql/internal/s4qlpb";

  // A commit object: everything one commit did.
  //
  // It is written and read as a stream: the header first, then the changes
  // and the annotations, one at a time.
  message CommitObject {
    // Exactly once, and first. A reader refuses a commit object without it.
    Header header = 1;
    // What the commit changed, in the order in which replay applies it.
    // A reader refuses the commit object if it does not know a change.
    repeated Change changes = 2;
    // What else is known about the commit. Replay does not use them, and a
    // reader may skip them.
    repeated Annotation annotations = 3;
  }

  // What the commit object is: its format, what came before it, and when it
  // was made.
  message Header {
    // The version of this format: 0 while the format is a draft. A reader
    // refuses a version it does not know. From version 1 on, the version is
    // raised when something is added that a reader must not ignore.
    uint32 version = 1;
    // The name of the commit object before this one: 32 bytes, all zero in
    // the first one.
    bytes parent = 2;
    // The wall-clock time of the commit, from the clock of the lease holder.
    // Replay does not use it.
    google.protobuf.Timestamp commit_time = 3;
  }

  // One change to the database.
  //
  // A Change holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader that finds
  // no known kind refuses the commit object: if the change holds an unknown
  // field, a newer version of s4ql wrote it; if it holds nothing, it is
  // malformed.
  message Change {
    // What the change is.
    oneof kind {
      // Creates a table.
      CreateTable create_table = 1;
      // Drops a table and all its rows.
      DropTable drop_table = 2;
      // Puts rows into a table.
      PutRows put_rows = 3;
      // Deletes rows from a table.
      DeleteRows delete_rows = 4;
    }
  }

  // Creates a table.
  message CreateTable {
    // The name of the table.
    string table = 1;
    // The columns, in their declared order.
    repeated Column columns = 2;
    // The primary-key columns, in key order, as positions in `columns`,
    // starting at 0.
    repeated uint32 primary_key = 3;
  }

  // One column of a table.
  message Column {
    // The name of the column.
    string name = 1;
    // The declared type of the column.
    Type type = 2;
  }

  // The declared type of a column. More are added as the roadmap grows.
  // A reader refuses a value it does not know.
  enum Type {
    // Never written. A reader refuses it.
    TYPE_UNSPECIFIED = 0;
    // The STRICT type INT.
    TYPE_INT = 1;
  }

  // Drops a table and all its rows.
  message DropTable {
    // The name of the table.
    string table = 1;
  }

  // Puts rows into a table. A row replaces any row with the same primary key.
  //
  // A PutRows is a batch that is small enough to hold in memory. A commit that
  // puts many rows into one table writes several batches.
  message PutRows {
    // The name of the table.
    string table = 1;
    // The rows. Each holds a value for every column, in the table's column
    // order.
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  // Deletes rows from a table.
  //
  // A DeleteRows is a batch, like a PutRows.
  message DeleteRows {
    // The name of the table.
    string table = 1;
    // The rows. Each holds only its primary-key values, in key order.
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  // One row: its values, in the order that the change gives.
  message Row {
    // The values.
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  // One SQLite value, in one of SQLite's five storage classes.
  //
  // A Value holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader refuses a
  // value with no known kind, as it does a change.
  message Value {
    // The storage class of the value, and the value.
    oneof kind {
      // A 64-bit signed integer.
      sint64 integer = 1;
      // A 64-bit IEEE 754 floating-point number.
      double real = 2;
      // Text. It is always valid UTF-8.
      string text = 3;
      // A blob.
      bytes blob = 4;
      // NULL.
      Null null = 5;
    }
  }

  // The SQL NULL. It has no fields.
  message Null {}

  // One thing that is known about a commit beyond its changes.
  //
  // A reader skips a kind of annotation it does not know.
  message Annotation {
    // What the annotation is.
    oneof kind {
      // One statement, as it ran.
      Statement statement = 1;
    }
  }

  // One statement, as it ran.
  message Statement {
    // The SQL text. It is always valid UTF-8.
    string sql = 1;
    // The parameters, by position, starting with parameter 1.
    repeated Value parameters = 2;
    // Non-deterministic inputs, such as the time the statement read, become
    // fields here when a slice of the roadmap first needs them.
  }

  Still open from before the review: what a reader does with a field it does not
  know outside the annotations.

  Anything more on the schema, or shall we settle that question next?

✻ Baked for 46s · done 10:24 AM

❯ PutRows -> InsertRows?                                                        

⏺ I'd not use InsertRows, because the message also carries updated rows. A file
  holds the net effect of a commit, so update t1 set v2 = 5 where v1 = 1 ends up
  as one full row with a key that already exists. In SQL, an insert of an
  existing key is an error, so the name would say the wrong thing for half of
  what the message does.

  If the aim is a word from SQL, SQLite has one with exactly this meaning:
  REPLACE. Its documentation describes REPLACE INTO as an alias for INSERT OR 
  REPLACE: the row is inserted, and a row with the same key is removed first.
  That is what replay does with each row.

  So the candidates are:

  - PutRows: the word from key-value stores. It is neutral, but it is not SQL.
  - ReplaceRows: SQLite's own word for "insert, or replace the row with the same
    key". Its weakness is that it sounds as if the row must already exist.
  - InsertRows: familiar, but wrong for updated rows.

  Splitting it into InsertRows for new keys and UpdateRows for existing ones
  would make each name exact. I'd not do that: the writer would have to track
  which is which, and replay gains nothing, since it does the same thing for
  both.

  I lean towards keeping PutRows, with ReplaceRows as the choice if you want
  SQLite's vocabulary. Names can still change later.

  PutRows, ReplaceRows, or InsertRows?

⏺ Yes, UpsertRows is better than my three. It means "insert, or update if the
  row exists", which is exactly what the message does, and it does not suggest
  that the row must already be there. It is renamed, pushed to the pull request,
  and recorded in docs/todo.md. The file passes Buf's lint.

  One note: the roadmap says "upserts" are rejected for now. That means SQL's on
  conflict clause, which is a statement feature. The message name is unrelated
  to it.

⏺ Agreed and done. DeleteRows now holds a list of Keys, and a Key is its own
  message: a value for every primary-key column, in key order. A Row then always
  means a full row. It is pushed to the pull request and recorded in
  docs/todo.md, and the file passes Buf's lint.

  A key holds a list of values and not a single value, because a primary key can
  span several columns.

  The whole file:

  // The format of an s4ql commit object.
  //
  // The format is a draft while s4ql is in development. Anything in it can
  // change, including field numbers and types, and a commit object written
  // in it carries version 0.
  //
  // From version 1 on, commit objects are kept forever, and every version of
  // s4ql must read every one ever written. Field numbers and field types then
  // never change, and a number that is no longer used is reserved. Names can

  // no known kind refuses the commit object: if the change holds an unknown
  // field, a newer version of s4ql wrote it; if it holds nothing, it is

  message Change {
    // What the change is.
    oneof kind {
      // Creates a table.
      CreateTable create_table = 1;

    // The declared type of the column.
    Type type = 2;
  }

  // The declared type of a column. More are added as the roadmap grows.
  // A reader refuses a value it does not know.
  enum Type {
    // Never written. A reader refuses it.
    TYPE_UNSPECIFIED = 0;
    // The STRICT type INT.
    TYPE_INT = 1;
  }

  // Drops a table and all its rows.
  message DropTable {
    // The name of the table.
    string table = 1;
  }

  // Inserts rows into a table. A row replaces any row with the same primary
  // key.
  //
  // An UpsertRows is a batch that is small enough to hold in memory. A commit
  // that upserts many rows into one table writes several batches.
  message UpsertRows {
    // The name of the table.
    string table = 1;
    // The rows.
    repeated Row rows = 2;
  }

  // Deletes rows from a table.
  //
  // A DeleteRows is a batch, like an UpsertRows.
  message DeleteRows {
    // The name of the table.
    string table = 1;
    // The primary keys of the rows to delete.
    repeated Key keys = 2;
  }

  // One row: a value for every column, in the table's column order.
  message Row {
    // The values.
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  // One primary key: a value for every primary-key column, in key order.
  message Key {
    // The values.
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  // One SQLite value, in one of SQLite's five storage classes.
  //
  // A Value holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader refuses a
  // value with no known kind, as it does a change.

  //

  message Annotation {

    oneof kind {

      Statement statement = 1;
    }
  }

    string sql = 1;
    // The parameters, by position, starting with parameter 1.

    // fields here when a slice of the roadmap first needs them.
  }

❯ see comment                                                                   

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ One unresolved thread.

  Comment 1 of 1, line 111, on the rows in UpsertRows, "Each holds a value for
  every column":

  ▎ Wouldn't we rather want only the columns that need changing? How do other 
  ▎ systems do it?

  How others do it, from the research note on log contents:

  ┌───────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      System       │                An updated row holds                 │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PostgreSQL        │ the full new row, with a marker for large values    │
  │ logical           │ that did not change                                 │
  │ replication       │                                                     │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ MySQL row-based   │ the full row by default; "minimal" images are an    │
  │ binlog            │ option, and the manual warns that they can make     │
  │                   │ replicas "silently diverge"                         │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SQLite changesets │ only the changed columns, with an "undefined"       │
  │                   │ marker that is distinct from NULL                   │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Spanner change    │ only the changed columns by default; the full new   │
  │ streams           │ row is an option                                    │
  ├───────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Debezium,         │ the full row                                        │
  │ CockroachDB       │                                                     │
  └───────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So both exist. The systems that send only changed columns all need a marker
  for "unchanged" that is separate from NULL.

  My assessment: your point has real weight, and I'd decide it later, not now.

  - The case for changed columns is size. If a row has a large text or blob and
    a commit changes a small counter in it, a full row stores the large value
    again in every such commit. With no snapshots in the first design, the log
    keeps all of them.
  - The cost is a second kind of row change. An inserted row is always full, so
    UpsertRows stays. Changed columns would be a new kind, UpdateRows, which
    says which columns it holds. By your own argument about the precondition, it
    is safe to apply.
  - Nothing in slice 1 can use it. There is no update statement yet, and a table
    has one column, which is its key. The first statement that changes part of
    a row comes with a later slice.

  We agreed that kinds of change follow the roadmap. I'd add this as an open
  question for the slice that brings update, with these findings, and leave
  UpsertRows as it is.

  Decide it with the update slice?

✻ Sautéed for 26s · done 10:51 AM

❯ Ok, make a note of it. But since we're calling it upsert already anyway, we   
  have some update design already. I lean towards the column position first,    
  then the values after                                                         

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Noted in docs/todo.md and pushed, and the thread is answered and resolved. The
  schema is unchanged, so I'm not printing it again.

  The note records it as a question for the slice that brings update, with your
  lean: a new kind of change that names the changed columns by their positions
  in the table first, and then holds their values. That shape has an advantage
  over SQLite's and Spanner's: because the columns are named, it needs no marker
  for "unchanged" that must be told apart from NULL.

  You're right that UpsertRows is already an update design. An updated row works
  today as a full row, and the changed-columns form would be a smaller way to
  store the same thing.

  All threads on the pull request are resolved. One question is left from before
  the review: what does a reader do with a field it does not know, outside the 
  annotations?

  For example, a later version adds "not null" to Column. By default an old
  reader skips the new field and creates the column without the constraint, so
  its cache silently differs from the log.

  - A: the reader refuses any commit object with an unknown field outside the
    annotations, and asks for a newer s4ql. The rule is mechanical.
  - B: we raise version whenever we add a field that must not be ignored. An old
    reader then refuses by the version. This depends on us remembering it every
    time.

  I recommend A.

  A or B?

✻ Brewed for 22s · done 10:53 AM

❯ I think we should add a column index to the row before the values             

⏺ That works, and I'd put the positions on the batch, not on each row:

  message UpsertRows {
    string table = 1;
    // The columns that each row holds a value for, as positions in the
    // table's columns, starting at 0. They always include the primary key.
    repeated uint32 columns = 2;
    repeated Row rows = 3;
  }

  Each row then holds its values in the order of columns.

  - All columns listed: the row is inserted, or replaces the row with the same
    key. This is every case in slice 1.
  - Some columns listed: the row with that key exists, and only the listed
    columns change. The key columns are always among them, because they say
    which row is meant.

  Why on the batch and not on each row: the rows that one statement changes
  nearly always change the same columns. update t1 set v2 = ? touches v2 in
  every row, and a bulk insert sets every column in every row. With the
  positions on the batch, they are stored once per megabyte. On each row, a
  million-row insert would repeat the same list a million times. Rows with
  different sets of columns go into different batches.

  The design also needs no marker for "unchanged", which SQLite and Spanner
  need, because a column that is not listed is simply not touched.

  One caution: only the "all columns" case can be exercised in slice 1, so the
  partial case stays untested until a slice brings update. The format is a
  draft, so that is acceptable.

  Add columns to UpsertRows, on the batch?

  message DeleteRows {

  }

  // One primary key: a value for every primary-key column, in key order.

    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  The two uses stay distinct: CreateTable.primary_key says which columns form
  the key, and DeleteRows.primary_keys holds the key values of the rows to

⏺ Both are done, pushed to the pull request, and recorded in docs/todo.md. The
  file passes Buf's lint. I renamed the message to PrimaryKey along with the
  field, as proposed.

  The whole file:

  // The format of an s4ql commit object.
  //
  // The format is a draft while s4ql is in development. Anything in it can
  // change, including field numbers and types, and a commit object written
  // in it carries version 0.
  //
  // From version 1 on, commit objects are kept forever, and every version of
  // s4ql must read every one ever written. Field numbers and field types then
  // never change, and a number that is no longer used is reserved. Names can
  // change.
  edition = "2024";

  package s4ql.v1;

  import "google/protobuf/timestamp.proto";

  option go_package = "maragu.dev/s4ql/internal/s4qlpb";

  // A commit object: everything one commit did.
  //
  // It is written and read as a stream: the header first, then the changes
  // and the annotations, one at a time.
  message CommitObject {
    // Exactly once, and first. A reader refuses a commit object without it.
    Header header = 1;
    // What the commit changed, in the order in which replay applies it.
    // A reader refuses the commit object if it does not know a change.
    repeated Change changes = 2;
    // What else is known about the commit. Replay does not use them, and a
    // reader may skip them.
    repeated Annotation annotations = 3;
  }

  // What the commit object is: its format, what came before it, and when it
  // was made.
  message Header {
    // The version of this format: 0 while the format is a draft. A reader
    // refuses a version it does not know. From version 1 on, the version is
    // raised when something is added that a reader must not ignore.
    uint32 version = 1;
    // The name of the commit object before this one: 32 bytes, all zero in
    // the first one.
    bytes parent = 2;
    // The wall-clock time of the commit, from the clock of the lease holder.
    // Replay does not use it.
    google.protobuf.Timestamp commit_time = 3;
  }

  // One change to the database.
  //
  // A Change holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader that finds
  // no known kind refuses the commit object: if the change holds an unknown
  // field, a newer version of s4ql wrote it; if it holds nothing, it is
  // malformed.
  message Change {
    // What the change is.
    oneof kind {
      // Creates a table.
      CreateTable create_table = 1;
      // Drops a table and all its rows.
      DropTable drop_table = 2;
      // Inserts rows into a table, or replaces the rows with the same keys.
      UpsertRows upsert_rows = 3;
      // Deletes rows from a table.
      DeleteRows delete_rows = 4;
    }
  }

  // Creates a table.
  message CreateTable {
    // The name of the table.
    string table = 1;
    // The columns, in their declared order.
    repeated Column columns = 2;
    // The primary-key columns, in key order, as positions in `columns`,
    // starting at 0.
    repeated uint32 primary_key = 3;
  }

  // One column of a table.
  message Column {
    // The name of the column.
    string name = 1;
    // The declared type of the column.
    Type type = 2;
  }

  // The declared type of a column. More are added as the roadmap grows.
  // A reader refuses a value it does not know.
  enum Type {
    // Never written. A reader refuses it.
    TYPE_UNSPECIFIED = 0;
    // The STRICT type INT.
    TYPE_INT = 1;
  }

  // Drops a table and all its rows.
  message DropTable {
    // The name of the table.
    string table = 1;
  }

  // Inserts rows into a table, or changes rows that are there.
  //
  // If `columns` lists every column of the table, each row is inserted, or
  // replaces the row with the same primary key. If it lists only some, each
  // row is there already, and only the listed columns change.
  //
  // An UpsertRows is a batch that is small enough to hold in memory. A commit
  // that upserts many rows into one table writes several batches, and rows
  // with different columns go into different batches.
  message UpsertRows {
    // The name of the table.
    string table = 1;
    // The columns that each row holds a value for, as positions in the
    // table's columns, starting at 0. They always include the primary-key
    // columns.
    repeated uint32 columns = 2;
    // The rows.
    repeated Row rows = 3;
  }

  // Deletes rows from a table.
  //
  // A DeleteRows is a batch, like an UpsertRows.
  message DeleteRows {
    // The name of the table.
    string table = 1;
    // The primary keys of the rows to delete.
    repeated PrimaryKey primary_keys = 2;
  }

  // One row: a value for each column that its UpsertRows lists, in that
  // order.
  message Row {
    // The values.
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  // One primary key: a value for every primary-key column, in key order.
  message PrimaryKey {
    // The values.
    repeated Value values = 1;
  }

  // One SQLite value, in one of SQLite's five storage classes.
  //
  // A Value holds no field outside `kind`, now or later. A reader refuses a
  // value with no known kind, as it does a change.
  message Value {
    // The storage class of the value, and the value.
    oneof kind {
      // A 64-bit signed integer.
      sint64 integer = 1;
      // A 64-bit IEEE 754 floating-point number.
      double real = 2;
      // Text. It is always valid UTF-8.
      string text = 3;
      // A blob.
      bytes blob = 4;
      // NULL.
      Null null = 5;
    }
  }

  // The SQL NULL. It has no fields.
  message Null {}

  // One thing that is known about a commit beyond its changes.
  //
  // A reader skips a kind of annotation it does not know.
  message Annotation {
    // What the annotation is.
    oneof kind {
      // One statement, as it ran.
      Statement statement = 1;
    }
  }

  // One statement, as it ran.
  message Statement {
    // The SQL text. It is always valid UTF-8.
    string sql = 1;
    // The parameters, by position, starting with parameter 1.
    repeated Value parameters = 2;
    // Non-deterministic inputs, such as the time the statement read, become
    // fields here when a slice of the roadmap first needs them.
  }

  All threads are resolved. The question from before the review is still open:
  what a reader does with a field it does not know outside the annotations. I
  recommend that it refuses the commit object.

  Anything more on the schema, or shall we settle that question next?

⏺ Recorded in docs/todo.md, and stated in the schema's comments. It is pushed to

  Only two comments changed, so I'm showing those and not the whole file again:

  // A commit object: everything one commit did.
  //
  // It is written and read as a stream: the header first, then the changes
  // and the annotations, one at a time.
  //
  // A reader refuses a commit object that holds a field it does not know,
  // anywhere outside the annotations: a newer version of s4ql wrote it, and
  // the field may change what replay must do. Inside an annotation, a reader
  // skips a field it does not know.
  message CommitObject {

    // The version of this format: 0 while the format is a draft. A reader
    // refuses a version it does not know. From version 1 on, the version is
    // raised when the meaning of the format changes without a new field.
    uint32 version = 1;

  The second one follows from the rule. A new field is now refused without any
  help from the version. The version is still needed for the other case, where
  what the bytes mean changes although no field is added.

  With that, nothing is open on the schema from my side, and every thread on the
  pull request is resolved.

✻ Baked for 37s · done 10:59 AM

❯ Yes, done for now.                                                            

⏺ Good. The schema is recorded in docs/todo.md, and that copy is what a builder
  works from. I'll leave pull request #4 open as a draft, so that you can
  comment inline again later. It is not to be merged.

  The spec still says "change file" and "evidence". Next: the spec's wording for
  the new terms.

  Four bullets need only the word swapped, "change file" to "commit object": "S3
  holds a log", "The manifest swap is the commit", "The cache is never ahead of
  S3", and "An unknown commit outcome is resolved from S3".

  Three bullets change a little more, and I propose this wording:

  ▎ The log is a linked list. Each commit object is named by the SHA-256 
  ▎ checksum of its content, with no metadata in the name, and records the name 
  ▎ of the commit object before it, its parent. The manifest points only to the 
  ▎ newest commit object. As with a branch in git, that one pointer identifies 
  ▎ the whole history, and a reader can verify every commit object against its 
  ▎ name.
  ▎
  ▎ Commit objects are row-based. A commit object records results, not 
  ▎ statements: the rows a commit inserted or changed, the keys of the rows it 
  ▎ deleted, and its schema changes, as the net effect of the commit. Replaying 
  ▎ the log applies these results and does not run SQL.
  ▎
  ▎ Statements are kept as annotations. Next to the results, a commit object 
  ▎ stores the SQL statements that produced them, with their parameters and 
  ▎ their non-deterministic inputs, such as the time and random seeds. They are 
  ▎ annotations, not the truth, and replay never depends on them.

  What is new in them, besides the terms: the word "parent" in the first, and
  "with their parameters" in the third.

  One more bullet, "Storage formats are backward and forward compatible", needs
  a real change, because we decided that an older reader refuses some things.
  I'll bring that one separately.

  Shall I write these seven into the spec?

⏺ All three are done.

  - Pull request #4 is closed, with a comment that points to where the schema
    lives. Its worktree and both branches are removed.
  - The schema is in docs/todo.md, under "Storage formats". I checked that this
    copy is identical to the final file in the pull request, all 193 lines. It
    is the only copy now, and the note says so. When a builder builds it, it
    becomes a real .proto file next to the code, and the to-do item is deleted.
  - The spec uses the new terms. The seven bullets are changed as proposed, and
    pushed. No "change file" or "evidence" is left in the spec.

  That leaves the one bullet I said needs a real change. It reads today:

  ▎ Storage formats are backward and forward compatible. A format stored in S3 
  ▎ can gain fields without breaking older or newer readers, in the style of 
  ▎ protocol buffers, though not necessarily with protocol buffers.

  That no longer matches what we decided: an older reader refuses some things,
  the encoding is chosen, and the formats are drafts for now. I propose to
  replace it with two bullets:

  ▎ Storage formats are compatible across versions. Every version of s4ql reads 
  ▎ what any older version wrote. An older version that meets something it does 
  ▎ not know skips it only where that cannot change the data, such as an 
  ▎ annotation. Otherwise it refuses, and asks for a newer version. Commit 
  ▎ objects are encoded with Protocol Buffers.
  ▎
  ▎ The storage formats are drafts until version 1. While s4ql is in 
  ▎ development, the formats can change in any way, and what is stored carries 
  ▎ version 0. The compatibility rule holds from version 1 on.

  Shall I write these two into the spec?

⏺ Both bullets are in the spec and pushed.

  That settles the three design questions that stood before the storage core:
  the lease and its clocks, a manifest swap with an unknown outcome, and the two
  formats. All of it is in the spec and in docs/todo.md.

  The next step is to build. I'd split the storage core into four pieces:

  1. The simulation harness. Turn the spike in internal/sim into the real one:
     the runtime interface, the lint, and every seed run twice in CI.
  2. The formats. The manifest and the commit object as code: the .proto file,
     the generated code, a strict reader and a stream writer, with fuzz tests
     and the CI checks on the schema.
  3. The object store. The interface, and a simulated store with
     compare-and-swap, create-if-absent, faults, and unknown outcomes.
  4. The core. Lease, commit, startup with catch-up, and the stop on a local
     failure, all under simulation from the first commit.

  Pieces 1 to 3 do not depend on each other. Piece 4 needs all three.

  One constraint: the weekly subscription is 91% used, and it resets on Friday
  at 21:00. Three builders in parallel would probably not finish before then.

  So I'd start piece 2 now with a single Opus builder, because it is small and
  freshly designed, and start pieces 1 and 3 after the reset.

  Start the formats now, or wait with everything until the reset?
